MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS

LECTURES BY BRIAN CONRAD, NOTES BY AARON LANDESMAN

This document consists of lectures notes from a course at Stanford University in Spring
quarter 2018, along with appendices written by Conrad as supplements to the lectures.
The goal is to cover the structure theory of semisimple compact connected Lie groups, an
excellent “test case” for the general theory of semisimple connected Lie groups (avoiding
many analytic difficulties, thanks to compactness). This includes a tour through the “clas-
sical (compact) groups”, the remarkable properties of maximal tori (especially conjugacy
thereof), some basic ideas related to Lie algebras, and the combinatorial concepts of root
system and Weyl group which lead to both a general structure theory in the compact
connected case as well as a foothold into the finite-dimensional representation theory of
such groups.
For a few topics (such as the manifold structure on G/H and basic properties of root
systems) we give a survey in the lectures and refer to the course text [BtD] for omitted
proofs. The homework assignments work out some important results (as well as instructive
examples), and also develop some topological background (such as covering space theory
and its relation to π1), so one should regard the weekly homework as an essential part of
the course. We largely avoid getting into the precise structure theory of semisimple Lie
algebras, and develop what we need with sl2 at one crucial step.
The main novelty of our approach compared to most other treatments of compact groups
is using techniques that adapt to linear algebraic groups over general fields: (i) much more
emphasis on the character and cocharacter lattices attached to a maximal torus T than on
their scalar extensions to R (identified with Lie(T)∗and Lie(T) respectively) (ii) extensive
use of torus centralizers and commutator subgroups, (iii) developing root systems over a
general field of characteristic 0 (especially allowing Q, not just R), as in [Bou2].
Please send any errata (typos, math errors, etc.) to conrad@math.stanford.edu.

1. BASICS OF TOPOLOGICAL GROUPS

1.1. First definitions and examples.

Definition 1.1. A topological group is a topological space G with a group structure such that
the multiplication map

m : G × G →G

and inversion map

i : G →G

are continuous.

Example 1.2. The open subset

GLn(R) ⊂Matn(R)

can be given the structure of a topological group via the usual multiplication and in-
version of matrices. We topologize it with the subspace topology (with Matn(R) given
the Euclidean topology as a finite-dimensional R-vector space). Matrix multiplication is
given in terms of polynomials, and similarly for inversion with rational functions whose
denominator is a power of the determinant (Cramer’s Formula).
Likewise, GLn(C) ⊂Matn(C) is a topological group.
In general, to give a coordinate-free description, for V a finite-dimensional vector space
over R or C, the open subset GL(V) ⊂End(V) of invertible linear endomorphisms is a
topological group with the subspace topology.

The following lemma is elementary to check:

Lemma 1.3. For topological groups G and G′, G × G′ with the product topology is a topological
group.

Example 1.4. We have an isomorphism of topological groups

R>0 × S1 →C× = GL1(C)
(r, w) �→rw.

Example 1.5. For G a topological group, any subgroup H ⊂G is a topological group with
the subspace topology. For us, this will be most useful for closed H ⊂G.

Example 1.6. For G = GLn(R), the special linear group

SLn(R) := {g ∈G : det g = 1}

is a closed subset of Matn(R).

Example 1.7. The orthogonal group

O(n) :=

Remark 1.8. In general, for g ∈O(n) each g(ei) is a unit vector, so g has bounded entries
as a matrix. Hence, O(n) is bounded in Matn(R), and it is also closed in Matn(R) (not only
in GLn(R)!) since the description g⊤g = 1n implies invertibilty of g, so O(n) is compact.

1.2. Non-compact analogues of O(n). Recall that a quadratic form q : V →R on a finite-
dimensional R-vector space V is a function given in linear coordinates by a degree-2
homogeneous polynomial ∑i≤j aijxixj (a condition that is clearly insensitive to linear
change of coordinates).

Lemma 1.9. For V a finite-dimensional vector space over R, we have a bijection

(1.1)

{symmetric bilinear B : V × V →R}

{quadratic forms q : V →R}

by the assignments B �→(qB : v �→B(v, v)) and

(1.2)

The main point of the proof is to check that each proposed construction lands in the
asserted space of objects and that they’re inverse to each other. This is left as an exercise.
The astute reader will check that this works well over any field not of characteristic 2.
Applying the preceding lemma with B taken to be the standard dot product on Rn, for
which qB is the usual norm-square, we have:

Definition 1.11. A quadratic form q is non-degenerate if the associated bilinear form Bq is a
perfect pairing, or equivalently its associated symmetric matrix (for a choice of basis of V)
is invertible.

Example 1.12. Let V = Rn. We can consider the form

qr,n−r(x) = x2

n.

The associated matrix B is a diagonal matrix whose first r entries are 1 and last n −r entries
are −1.
The Gram-Schmidt process (adapted to non-degenerate quadratic forms that might have
some values ≤0) plus a bit of care implies that every (V, q) over R that is non-degenerate is
isomorphic to (Rn, qr,n−r) for a unique 0 ≤r ≤n. The idea is to build an orthonormal-like
basis using that all positive real numbers have square roots, and the uniqueness needs

some care since there is no intrinsic “maximal positive-definite subspace” (there are many
such). We call the resulting pair (r, n −r) the signature of the associated quadratic form q.

Definition 1.13. For q a non-degenerate quadratic form on a finite-dimensional R-vector
space V, we define

O(q) := {g ∈GL(V) : q(gv) = q(v)} ⊂GL(V),

which is a closed subset of GL(V).

Remark 1.14. When q has signature (r, n −r), we denote O(q) by O(r, n −r). For 0 < r < n,
O(r, n −r) is non-compact. One way to see this is to note that it contains O(1, 1) as a
closed subgroup (namely the subgroup that is the identity on all but one of the “positive”
ei’s and on all but one of the “negative” ej’s), yet O(1, 1) is non-compact because explicit
computation yields

Disconnectedness is somewhat annoying in the setting of Lie groups because it obstructs
the use of Lie algebra techniques later on, as Lie algebras will only tell us about the
connected component of the identity.

1.3. Connectedness. We’ll now discuss questions of connectedness, beginning with some
examples (to be revisited more fully later).

Question 1.16. Is GLn(R) connected?

The answer is negative since we have a surjective continuous determinant map

det : GLn(R) →R×,

and R× is not connected. So, we might ask whether

GL+
n (R) = {g ∈GLn(R) : det g > 0}

is connected. Since GL+
n (R) maps under the deteterminant map onto R>0, we actually
have an isomorphism of topological groups (check homeomorphsm!)

GLn(R) ≃R>0 ⋉SLn(R).

Hence, GL+
n (R) is connected if and only if SLn(R) is connected. It turns out SLn(R) is
connected, though this requires some work that will be taken up later.

Remark 1.17. We saw O(n) = SO(n) ⋊(Z/2Z) is disconnected, but later we will show
that SO(n) is connected. To appreciate this, we note that for 0 < r < n the non-compact
SO(r, n −r) is always disconnected (for reasons related to orientation issues that are
addressed in Appendix D, though the case n = 2 and r = 1 was seen explicitly above).

Example 1.18. The group GLn(C) is connected, and we’ll see why in HW2.

Definition 1.19. For G a topological group, let G0 denote the connected component of
1 ∈G.

The subset G0 ⊂G is closed, as for the connected component of any point in a topological
space.

Lemma 1.20. The closed subset G0 ⊂G is a subgroup.

Proof. The point is that in the commutative diagram

(1.3)

G0 × G0
G × G

G0
G

m

the point (1, 1) ∈G0 × G0 maps to 1 ∈G0 and m is continuous (so it carries the connected
G0 × G0 onto a connected subset of G).
□

Construction 1.21. Consider the open subset GL(V) ⊂End(V) inside an associative finite-
dimensional R-algebra. This is the collection of 2-sided units, and we know from linear
algebra that a left inverse is the same thing as a right inverse. We now generalize this
considerably. Let A be a finite-dimensional associative algebra over R or C (with 1 ∈A);
a special case is End(V). Suppose a ∈A admits a left inverse: an element a′ ∈A with
a′a = 1A. Then, we claim:

Lemma 1.22. The element a′ is also a right inverse; i.e., aa′ = 1A.

Proof. Consider the natural map

ϕ : A →Endv.sp.(A)
x �→ℓx,

where

ℓx : A →A
α �→xα.

The map ϕ is a map of associative algebras (check!), and we have (by associativity)

ℓa′ ◦ℓa = ℓa′a = ℓ1A = idA .

This implies ℓa ◦ℓa′ = idA by linear algebra. Applying this to 1A ∈A, we have aa′ =
1A.
□

Now it is unambiguous to define

A× = {a ∈A : a is invertible } ⊂End(A),

and this is a group by associativity. Moreover, it is an open subset of End(A) because we
claim that the inclusion
A× ⊂A ∩End(A)×

is an equality. That is, if ℓa : A →A is a linear isomorphism then we claim that a is
invertible. Indeed, by surjectivity we have ℓa(a′) = 1A for some a′ ∈A, which is to say
aa′ = 1A, so we also know a′a = 1A and hence a ∈A×.

Example 1.23. The quaternions are a specific 4-dimensional associative R-subalgebra

H ⊂Mat2(C).

This is discussed in detail in Appendix A It is very different from the 4-dimensional
associative R-subalgebra Mat2(R) because (as is shown in Appendix A) we have H× =
H −{0}! That is, H is a division algebra. That appendix discusses H in concrete terms,
including how to describe inversion. This example will be rather important for other key
examples in the course.

1.4. Quotients. Let G be a topological group and H ⊂G a subgroup (not necessarily
normal). Consider the map

π : G →G/H,

where the latter has the quotient topology (so a subset U ⊂G/H is open by definition when
π−1(U) is open).
Here is an important property that is not generally true of surjective quotient maps of
topological spaces.

Lemma 1.24. The continuous map π is open. That is, if Ω⊂G is open then π(Ω) is also open.

Proof. We have

π−1(π(Ω)) =
�

(Ω· h) ,

which is a union of open sets, hence open.
□

It is an important fact, left as an exercise to check, that the collection of open sets U in
G/H is in bijection with the collection of open sets Ωin G stable under right-multiplication
by H via the operations
U �→π−1(U),
Ω�→π(Ω).

Corollary 1.25. For any second such pair (G′, H′), the natural continuous bijection

Proof. The content is to show that the inverse map is continuous, and this is done by
applying the preceding general characterization of open sets in such quotient spaces via
open sets in the source that are invariant under suitable right-multiplications.
□

Remark 1.26. To appreciate how fortunate we are that the preceding corollary holds,
we warn that it is not generally true that the formation of a topological quotient space
commutes with the formation of direct products (beyond the quotient group setting as
above). To make an example, consider the quotient map

π : X = Q ↠Y = X/(n ∼0)n∈Z
that crushes Z to a point, where Q has its usual totally disconnected “archimedean”
topology as a subset of R. It turns out that if we form a direct product against Q, the
natural continuous bijection

(X × Q)/((n, q) ∼(0, q))n∈Z →Y × Q


8
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

is not a homeomorphism. A justification is given in [Brown, Example, p. 111] (where Y
above is denoted as Q/Z, not to be confused with the group-theoretic quotient!), whereas in
[Brown, 4.3.2] it is shown that such a phenomenon does not arise if one works with locally
compact Hausdorff spaces, such as replacing Q with R in the preceding construction.

Corollary 1.27. For H ⊂G normal, we have that G/H is a topological group.

Proof. This is immediate from Corollary 1.25 with G′ = G and H′ = H (using continuity
of the inverse of the bijection there in order to verify continuity of multiplication on
G/H).
□

The first interesting example of G/H as a topological group is:

Example 1.28. We have an isomorphism

R/Z →S1

t �→e2πit

To illustrate the importance of considering G/H topologically with H not necessarily
normal, consider a continuous action

G × X →X

of a topological group G on a topological space X. For a point x0 ∈X, consider its stabilizer

Gx0 := StabG(x0) ⊂G.

The G-action on x0 gives a continuous bijection

G/Gx0 →G · x0 ⊂X

onto the G-orbit of x0 equipped with the subspace topology from X.
Is this a homeomorphism? The answer is negative in general, but we will see that it is
affirmative in nice situations (and this will be upgraded to respect C∞-structures later on).
Here is one case where the homeomorphism property can be verified directly, and is of
immediate utility:

Example 1.29. Fix n ≥2, and let G = SO(n). For X = Rn with its usual G-action and
x0 = e1 the first basis vector, we have the orbit G.x0 = Sn−1 (since n ≥2; check!). The
G-stabilizer of e1 is SO(n −1) (on the space spanned by e2, . . . , en); make sure to check this
is really correct (i.e., the determinant-1 group SO(n −1), not O(n −1) or worse).
Thus, we have a continuous bijection

SO(n)/ SO(n −1) →Sn−1.

But the source is compact and the target is Hausdorff, so by everyone’s favorite point-
set topology exercise this is a homeomorphism! Since Sn−1 is connected (as n ≥2),
this will underlie a later inductive proof of connectedness of SO(n) beginning with the
connectedness of SO(1) = 1.

2. FURTHER CLASSES OF COMPACT GROUPS

Definition 2.1. A Lie group is a topological group with a C∞manifold structure so that
m : G × G →G and i : G →G are C∞.

Remark 2.2. See Appendix B for why the smoothness of inversion is automatic.

Definition 2.3. Let V be a complex vector space. A hermitian form on V is a map

h : V × V →C

that is sesquilinear (i.e., bi-additive and C-linear in the first slot but conjugate-linear in the
second slot) and satisfies h(w, v) = h(v, w).

Remark 2.4. On Cn, we define the “standard” hermitian form to be

⟨v, w⟩std = ∑
j
vjwj

(denoted ⟨v, w⟩std,n if we need to specify n, and sometimes just as ⟨·, ·⟩if the context
makes it clear). This particular hermitian form is also non-degenerate, meaning that the
conjugate-linear map

V →V∗

v �→h (·, v)

is an isomorphism (equivalently injective, by C-dimension reasons).

Remark 2.5. For h a hermitian form, as a consequence of the conjugate-symmetry we have
h(v, v) ∈R.

Remark 2.6. Next time, we’ll show that for any non-degenerate hermitian h on V there is
some (ordered) basis in which it looks like
∑
j
εjvjwj

with εj = ±1.

Definition 2.7. If (V, h) is non-degenerate, define

U(h) := {g ∈GL(V) : h(gv, gw) = h(v, w) for all v, w ∈V} .

Because h is non-degenerate, it is easy to check that any g ∈End(V) preserving h in this
way has trivial kernel and so is automatically in GL(V). Thus, the definition of U(h)
is unaffected by replacing GL(V) with End(V) (similarly to what we have seen for the
definition of O(q) for non-degenerate quadratic forms q on finite-dimensional R-vector
spaces), ensuring that U(h) is closed in End(V), not just closed in GL(V); this will be very
important.
Using V = Cn and h = ⟨·, ·⟩std,n yields the group

U(n) := U(⟨·, ·⟩std,n) =
�
g ∈GLn(C) : g⊤g = 1n
�
.

Note that as a condition on matrix entries of g these are some complicated quadratic rela-
tions in the real and imaginary parts; it is not “complex-algebraic”, due to the intervention
of complex conjugation.

Remark 2.8. HW1, Exercise 2 shows that the subset O(n) ⊂GLn(R) is a closed C∞

submanifold. One can check that the same method applies to show that the subset
U(n) ⊂GLn(C) ⊂GL2n(R) is a closed C∞submanifold (not a complex submanifold of
GLn(C) since the defining equations involve complex conjugation).

Lemma 2.9. For hermitian h on a (finite-dimensional, as always) C-vector space V and g ∈
GL(V) we have h(gv, gw) = h(v, w) for all v, w ∈V if and only if h(gv, gv) = h(v, v) for all
v ∈V.

Proof. We essentially want to reconstruct the hermitian form from the values on the diago-
nal. Observe

h (v + w, v + w) = h(v, v) + h(w, w) + h(v, w) + h(w, v)
= h(v, v) + h(w, w) + 2Re (h(v, w)) .

Similarly, using iv in place of v and using C-linearity in the first slot, we recover Im(h(v, w)).
This shows we can recover the hermitian form from its values on the diagonal and con-
cludes the proof.
□

Since the restriction of ⟨·, ·⟩std,n to pairs (v, v) for v ∈Cn coincides with the usual
length-squared on Cn = R2n, we obtain:

Corollary 2.10. We have

g ∈GLn(C) : ||gv||2 = ||v||2 for all v ∈Cn = R2n�

Proof. This follows from Lemma 2.9.
□

This corollary shows that the g(ej) are unit vectors, so the closed subset U(n) ⊂Matn(C)
is also bounded and hence compact.

Remark 2.12. We will later see that SU(n) is a “compact cousin” of SLn(R).

2.1. Compact forms of symplectic groups. Next, we’ll define a compact analogue of
symplectic groups by using “quaternionic hermitian” forms. First, let’s recall what a
symplectic group is. Recall that a bilinear form ψ on a finite-dimensional vector space
V over a field k is called alternating if ψ(v, v) = 0 for all v ∈V (which implies the skew-
symmetry condition ψ(w, v) = −ψ(v, w) by working with (v + w, v + w), and is equivalent
to skew-symmetry when char(k) ̸= 2).

Definition 2.13. A symplectic form ψ on a finite-dimensional vector space V over a field k is
an alternating bilinear form ψ : V × V →k that is non-degenerate, meaning that the linear
map

V →V∗

v �→ψ(v, ·)

is an isomorphism (equivalently injective, by dimension reasons).

Definition 2.16. The symplectic group of a symplectic space (V, ψ) is

Sp(ψ) = {g ∈GL(V) : ψ(gv, gw) = ψ(v, w) for all v, w ∈V} .

In the case of V = k2n and the standard ψ, we denote it as Sp2n(k).

Appendix A shows the following facts:

Definition 2.23. Define the “standard quaternionic-hermitian form”

⟨·, ·⟩: Hn × Hn →H

(v, w) �→∑

(Note the order of multiplication in the sum matters since the vector entries are in H.)
We have no need for a general theory of quaternionic-hermitian spaces, so rather than
make a more abstract definition we will just focus on this particular construction.

Lemma 2.24. The construction ⟨·, ·⟩in Definition 2.23 satisfies the following properties

(1) It is sesquilinear, meaning ⟨v, hw⟩= ⟨v, w⟩h for h ∈H, and moreover ⟨w, v⟩= ⟨v, w⟩.
(2) It is non-degenerate, meaning ⟨·, v⟩= 0 if and only if v = 0.
(3) It is positive-definite, meaning ⟨v, v⟩≥0 with equality if and only if v = 0.

The proof is left as an exercise. It is easy to check that ⟨v, v⟩is the usual squared-length
on Hn = R4n, so

||v|| :=
�

⟨v, v⟩

coincides with the usual notion of length on R4n.
We can now define the compact analogue of Sp2n(R):

Definition 2.25. Define

Sp(n) := {g ∈GLn(H) : ⟨gv, gw⟩= ⟨v, w⟩for all v, w ∈Hn}

Since any g ∈Sp(n) takes unit vectors to unit vectors, the matrix entries of such g
are bounded (for the notion of length on Hn). Moreover, the non-degeneracy of ⟨·, ·⟩
implies similarly to what we have seen for O(q) with non-degenerate q and U(h) for
non-degenerate h that in the definition of Sp(n) it is harmless to replace GLn(H) with
Matn(H). i.e., invertibility is automatic. Thus, Sp(n) is closed in the finite-dimensional
R-vector space Matn(H), so by boundedness it is compact.

Remark 2.26. A quaternion has “three imaginary parts”, and we can adap the trick used
in the case of hermitian forms over C with sesquilinearity to obtain

HW2 shows that Sp(n) is a C∞submanifold of GL2n(C), and [BtD, (1.11)] shows that
Sp(n) = U(2n) ∩GLn(H) inside GL2n(C) (exhibiting compactness in another way).

3. CONNECTEDNESS AND VECTOR FIELDS

3.1. Indefinite hermitian forms. Let’s first address a loose end from last time. Let h :
V × V →C be a hermitian form on a finite-dimensional C-vector space V. This yields two
maps, v �→h(·, v) and v �→h(v, ·). The former map sends v to a linear functional on V but
the actual map V →V∗is conjugate-linear. The latter map sends v to a conjugate-linear
functional, so it takes values in the space V∗of conjugate-linear forms on V (this is also
the linear dual of the space V obtained by extending scalars from C to C via the complex
conjugation map C →C), but the actual map V →V∗is C-linear.


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
15

These two maps
V →V∗, V →V∗

are “conjugate” to each other in a way that we leave to your imagination, so in particular
one is bijective if and only if the other is. The case when bijectivity holds is precisely our
definition from last time of h being non-degenerate.

Example 3.1. Let V = Cn, n = r + s with r, s ≥0. Then we define a hermitian form hr,s by:

hr,s(v, w) =
r
∑
j=1
vjwj −
s
∑
j=1
vr+jwr+j

This is easily checked to be non-degenerate.

Given any hermitian form h on V, we may restrict h to the diagonal

{(v, v) | v ∈V} ⊆V × V

to get an R-valued quadratic form qh : v �→h(v, v) = h(v, v) on the underlying real
vector space of V. Recall from the previous lecture that we can recover h from qh, so if
qh = 0 then h = 0. It is easy to check that h is non-degenerate as a hermitian form if and
only if qh is non-degenerate as an R-valued quadratic form. The examples above give
quadratic forms on the underlying real vector space of V with signature (2r, 2s). Hence,
by well-definedness of signature, (Cn, hr,s) ≃(Cn′, hr′,s′) if and only if r = r′ and s = s′.
Now, we have:

Proposition 3.2. Every non-degenerate hermitian space (V, h) is isomorphic to some (Cn, hr,s)
with r + s = n for a uniquely determined r, s.

Proof. Since h is non-zero, the quadratic form qh is non-zero. Thus, there exists some
v ∈V with h(v, v) ∈R×. Scale v by R>0 so that h(v, v) = ±1. Non-degeneracy of h
implies that (Cv)⊥:= {w ∈V | h(w, v) = 0} has a non-degenerate restriction for h and
that Cv ⊕(Cv)⊥= V. Then, we may replace (V, h) by ((Cv)⊥, h|(Cv)⊥) and induct on
dimension.
□

Note that this proof is essentially the Gram-Schmidt process as in the positive-definite
case, but some additional care is necessary: in the Gram-Schmidt process, we could choose
the nonzero vector v arbitrarily, but here we must ensure that qh(v) ̸= 0 so that the non-
degeneracy is preserved upon passage to the appropriate orthogonal hyperplane (an issue
that is automatic in the positive-definite case).

3.2. Some more topology. We will use the following convention for manifolds in this
course: all manifolds (whether topological or C∞or analytic) are assumed to be Hausdorff,
paracompact, and second-countable. (In the presence of the Hausdorff and paracompact-
ness hypotheses, the condition of M being second-countable is equivalent to having a
countable set of connected components.)
These mild conditions ensure the existence of C∞partitions of unity, so we can define
things like �

M ω for a differential form ω (when M is oriented) and other such construc-
tions.
Here is a useful point-set topological fact that will often be invoked without comment in
later work with coset spaces:


16
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Proposition 3.3. Let G be a topological group, with H ⊆G a subgroup. If H is closed then G/H
is Hausdorff.

Proof. Recall that a topological space X is Hausdorff if and only if the image of the diagonal
map ∆: X →X × X is closed in X × X.
Since (G × G)/(H × H) →(G/H) × (G/H) is a homeomorphism (as we discussed last
time), so G × G →(G/H) × (G/H) is a topological quotient map, it suffices to check that
the preimage of ∆(G/H) in G × G is closed. This is the set {(g, g′) ∈G × G | g−1g′ ∈H},
which is the preimage of the closed subset H ⊆G under the continuous map G × G →G
given by (g, g′) �→g−1g′, so it is closed.
□

Remark 3.4. The converse (that if G/H is Hausdorff then H is closed) is also true (though
useless for us) and is left as an easy exercise.

We will use coset spaces to prove that various compact Lie groups seen earlier are
connected. Connectedness results are very important in Lie theory: since the Lie algebra
captures infinitesimal information at the identity, we can only hope to extract from it
information about the connected component of the identity. The group of connected com-
ponents of a Lie group can be any discrete group (also countable under our countabillity
convention on manifolds), so without connectedness assumptions we are confronted with
all of the difficulties of general (or “just” countable) abstract groups.
Here is our first connectedness argument, which we sketched last time. It is based on
the method of transitive orbits on a connected space.

Example 3.5. We previously constructed a continuous bijection ϕ : SO(n)/ SO(n −1) →
Sn−1 for n ≥2 by studying the orbit of e1 ∈Rn under the standard action of SO(n). Note
that SO(n −1) is the stabilizer of e1 in SO(n). (Some Euclidean geometry is involved in
proving that the SO(n)-orbit of e1 is the entire unit sphere Sn−1, assuming n ≥2.) Since
SO(n) and therefore SO(n)/ SO(n −1) is compact and Sn−1 is Hausdorff, the continuous
bijection ϕ is actually a homeomorphism. We will see later on that it is actually a C∞

isomorphism, once we discuss how to endow coset spaces like SO(n)/ SO(n −1) with C∞

structures.
In HW2, you will show that if G/H is connected and H is connected, then G is con-
nected. Therefore, we may use the homeomorphism ϕ to show by induction that SO(n)
is connected for all n because of connectedness of SO(1) which is the trivial group
{+1} ⊆O(1) = {±1}. This method for n = 2 recovers the familiar isomorphism
SO(2) ≃S1.

Note that this argument would not work (and in fact, the conclusion is not always true)
for SO(q) for indefinite non-degenerate quadratic forms q over R. This is because such
groups do not preserve the unit ball, but rather preserve the set of length-1 vectors with
respect to some pseudo-metric. Such a “unit pseudo-ball” is typically disconnected (and
non-compact), such as a hyperbola in the plane or a hyperboloid with 2 sheets in R3.
The technique of argument in the preceding example is robust:

Example 3.6. HW2 uses a similar argument with S2n−1 ⊆Cn to show SU(n) is connected
for all n ≥1 by using an induction whose base case is the case n = 1 with SU(1) = {1} ⊆


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
17

U(1) = S1. In the case n = 2 this yields a homeomorphism

SU(2) =

−β

�α

Example 3.7. In HW2, we similarly use the unit ball S4n−1 ⊆Hn to show that Sp(n) is
connected for all n ≥1, using the base case Sp(1) = {h ∈H× | N(h) = 1} = SU(2).

How might we show (if true?) a similar connectedness result for the non-compact
groups SLn(R) and Sp2n(R)? We have the lucky fact, shown in HW1, that there is a
diffeomorphism SLn(R) ≃SO(n) × RN for a suitable N (warning: this is not a group
isomorphism!). This shows that SLn(R) is connected.
The method of studying an appropriate group action and inducting using stabilizers still
works in these non-compact cases, but we do not have a natural action of these groups on
spheres (the geometry of their natural action on a Euclidean space does not preserve the
Euclidean structure), so we must use a more sophisticated space (e.g. Grassmannians or
flag varieties). Also, in the non-compact setting we cannot use the trick that a continuous
bijection from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a homeomorphism, so to show that
the relevant continuous bijection G/StabG(x) →X is a homeomorphism onto Gx under
some mild topological hypotheses on G and X requires some more work.
Since matrix groups are such a ubiquitous source of examples of Lie groups in practice,
on the theme of connectedness we can’t resist mentioning the following interesting fact
(that we will never use in this course):

Theorem 3.8. For G ⊂GLn(R) a ”Zariski-closed” subgroup (i.e., a group given by the zero-set
of some polynomials in R[xij, 1/ det]), π0(G) is always finite.

Remark 3.9. In fact, the group setting is irrelevant: it is a general theorem of Whitney that
any zero locus of polynomials in any RN has only finitely many connected components:
for a proof see [Mil, App. A] which rests on [AF, §1, Lemma].

Note that it is possible that G in Theorem 3.8 is Zariski-connected (in the sense that it does
not admit a separation whose two members are defined by polynomial equations) but the
topological space G(R) is disconnected for its usual topology. This is the case with GLn(R)
(essentially because using the sign of the determinant to explain the disconnectedness
is not a “polynomial condition”). In addition, the groups SO(r, n −r) ⊂GLn(R) are
always Zariski-connected even though they are disconnected for the usual topology
whenever 0 < r < n. On the other hand, O(n) ⊂GLn(R) is not Zariski-connected since
the determinant being equal to 1 or being equal to −1 defines disjoint complementary
non-empty (closed and open) subsets.
A striking application of the fact that groups such as GLn(R) are Zariski-connected is
that if we are trying to prove a ”purely algebraic” fact for them then we can still profitably
use Lie-algebra methods (because there is a purely algebro-geometric formulation of the
Lie algebra, which however lies beyond the level of this course). In other words, certain
disconnected Lie groups can still be studied via Lie-algebra methods for the purpose of
sufficiently algebraic questions (provided that a weaker kind of connectedness, called


18
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Zariski-connectedness, holds). For someone who knows only analytic geometry and not
algebraic geometry, this must sound very bewildering!

3.3. Left-invariant vector fields. As a first step towards developing Lie algebras, we now
introduce the notion of left-invariant vector field.
Let G be a Lie group. We define Lie(G) = g := T1(G), i.e. the tangent space of the
point 1 ∈G. Let X be a ”set-theoretic” vector field on G, i.e. a collection of elements
X(g) ∈Tg(G) for all g ∈G. (This is the same thing as a set-theoretic section to the structure
map TG →G for the tangent bundle, for those who prefer such language.)

Definition 3.10. A set-theoretic vector field X on a Lie group G is left-invariant if for all
g, g′ ∈G, the natural linear isomorphism dℓg(g′) : Tg′(G) ≃Tgg′(G) carries X(g′) to
X(gg′), where ℓg : G →G is the diffeomorphism x �→gx given by left translation by g.

By the Chain Rule and the fact that ℓgg′ = ℓg ◦ℓg′, it is equivalent to say that there is
a vector v ∈g with X(g) = dℓg(v) for all g ∈G. Thus, we obtain a linear isomorphism
between g and the R-vector space of left-invariant vector fields on G by sending v ∈g to
�v : g �→dℓg(v). On HW2 we will show that such �v are actually C∞vector fields, so we will
never care about ”set-theoretic” vector fields ever again.

Example 3.11. For the open submanifold G = GLn(R) ⊆Matn(R), we have

Lie(G) = T1(Matn(R))

yet canonically Matn(R) ≃T1(Matn(R)) as a special case of the general canonical isomor-
phism V ≃Tv(V) for the tangent space at any point v in a finite-dimensional R-vector
space V viewed as a smooth manifold (this is just the “directional derivative at a point”
construction). Thus, we have a canonical isomorphism

Matn(R) ≃gln(R) := Lie(GLn(R)),

given by (aij) �→∑i,j aij∂xi,j|1. (In more coordinate-free terms, this says that canonically
End(V) ≃Lie(GL(V)) as R-vector spaces.)
This is made more explicit in HW2, where the next proposition is addressed:

Proposition 3.12. The following two statements hold:

(i) ∂xij|1n extends to the left-invariant vector field ∑k xki∂xkj.

(ii) For A, B in Matn(R), [ �A, �B](1) = AB −BA where [·, ·] denotes the commutator operation
on smooth global vector fields on a manifold.

4. LIE ALGEBRAS

We start with an extended parenthetical remark.

Remark 4.1 (Finite groups of Lie type and the relation to algebraic geometry). There is
a link between connected (complex) Lie groups and certain finite groups (called “finite
groups of Lie type”) such as SLn(Fq), Sp2n(Fq), etc. The idea is to use algebraic geometry
over Fq, applied to G(Fq), where G is the corresponding affine group variety (which is a
“matrix group” such as SLn or Sp2n that we view as defined by “equations over Z which
have nothing to do with Fq).


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
19

We view G(Fq) as a finite subgroup of something with much richer geometric structure:

G(Fq) ⊂∪n≥1G(Fqn) = G(Fq),

where G(Fq) is a smooth affine variety over Fq (whatever that means) and as such thereby
has a useful notion of “tangent space” at each point, such as Te(G(Fq)) on which one can
(with enough algebro-geometric technique) make a version of the “left-invariant vector
field” construction to define a version of [·, ·] on the tangent space at the identity. Even
though G(Fq) has no geometric structure, in many cases G(Fq) has a reasonable notion of
connectedness, and one can really harness that together with the Lie algebra on its tangent
space at the identity to deduce structural information that in turn tells us non-obvious
things about G(Fq)!
The link to Lie groups is that we have maps of commutstive rings

(4.1)

Z

C
Fq

and one can use these to relate Lie groups over C to corresponding algebraic groups “over
Z” (in the sense of schemes), and then over Fq via “scalar extension”.
One nice example application of this link is the following.

Example 4.2. There are formulas for the size of G(Fq) as a universal polynomial in q for
various G (e.g., the case of SLn is rather classical), but what conceptual explanation may be
found for the existence of such universal formulas (i.e., something better than: compute
the size and hope it is a universal formula in q)? A very long story going through a lot of
algebraic geometry provides such a conceptual explanation for the universal formula by
relating it to the purely topological invariants

H∗(G (C) , C)

that have nothing to do with any specific q!

Coming back to Lie groups G, we know inversion is automatically C∞when m is, as
shown in Appendix B. More precisely, that appendix shows that for g = Te(G),

is negation.

Remark 4.4. We recorded last time that for the open submanifold G = GLn(R) ⊂Matn(R)
canonically identifying g with Matn(R), the operation [·, ·] is the usual commutator. This
is proved by a velocity vector calculation in §C.1, where it is noted that the exact same
argument works for two other such Lie groups, namely the open submanifolds GLn(C) ⊂
Matn(C) and GLn(H) ⊂Matn(H) (with the corresponding commutator on those matrix
algebras).
This is very useful once we show that [·, ·]G is functorial in the group G, since the groups
G we care about will typically be closed subgroups of GLn(R), GLn(C), or GLn(H) (so we
can then compute the bracket on such Lie(G) in terms of matrix commutators).

4.1. Definition of Lie algebras.

Definition 4.5. A Lie algebra over a field k is a k-vector space g equipped with a k-bilinear
map

[·, ·] : g × g →g

so that

(1) [·, ·] is alternating, meaning [X, X] = 0 for all X (which implies [X, Y] = −[Y, X],
and is equivalent to this in characteristic not 2).


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
21

(2) [·, ·] satisfies the Jacobi identity:

[X, [Y, Z]] + [Y, [Z, X]] + [Z, [X, Y]] = 0.

Warning 4.6.

�
The operation [·, ·] is usually not associative, as can be seen by the Jacobi
identity. Further, there is typically no unit for the bracket operation (aside from silly cases
such as g = 0).

4.2. Examples of Lie Algebras.

Example 4.7. The cross product on R3 is isomorphic to Lie(SO(3)): see Exercise 2 on HW3.

Example 4.8. Suppose A is an associative k-algebra with identity. Then
�
a′, a
�
:= a′a −aa′

is easily checked to be a Lie algebra structure. As a special case, one can take A = Endk(V)
for V any k-vector space (even infinite-dimensional).

Example 4.9. By design of [·, ·]G, the injective R-linear map

g �→EndR (C∞(G))
v �→�v

is compatible with [·, ·] when using Example 4.8 on the target.

4.3. Functoriality of brackets.

Definition 4.10. For any given Lie group, we let the lower-case fraktur font denote the
corresponding Lie algebra. So for example,

GLn(R) ⇝gln(R), U(n) ⇝u(n), SLn(R) ⇝sln(R), Sp2n(R) ⇝sp2n(R).

The notion of homomorphism of Lie algebras is the natural one (a map of vector spaces
compatible with the Lie bracket). We say g is a Lie subalgebra of h is there is an injective
homomorphism of Lie algebras g →h.

Question 4.11. Is [·, ·]G on g functorial in G? That is, for f : G →H a map of Lie groups,
is d f (e) : g →h a homomorphism of Lie algebras?

Of course the answer is yes, but it is not obvious because we can’t “push forward” vector
fields. To prove this, we’ll develop a more local description of [·, ·].
Here are some side remarks, for general cultural awareness.

Remark 4.12. Every Lie algebra g over a field k is naturally a Lie subalgebra of an associa-
tive k-algebra U(g) with identity, known as the universal enveloping algebra. For more on
this, see [Bou1, Ch. I, §2.7, Cor. 2].

Remark 4.13. Every finite-dimensional Lie algebra g over a field k is a Lie subalgebra of
some gln(k) := Matn(k) (equipped with the commutator Lie algebra structure as for any
associative k-algebra). This result, called Ado’s Theorem, lies quite deep (so it would be
incorrect to think it is much Ado about nothing). It is proved at the very end of [Bou1,
Ch. I].

We now return to showing the bracket is functorial. First, we have an important
reformulation of the Jacobi identity, resting on:


22
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Definition 4.14. Let g be a Lie algebra over a field k. For any X ∈g we get a k-linear map

[X, ·] : g →g

and so a k-linear map

adg : g →Endk(g)

X �→[X, ·] .

It is a pleasant exercise to check that an alternating bilinear [·, ·] : g × g →g satisfies the
Jacobi identity if and only if the associated map adg built from [·, ·] as above intertwines
the bracket on the source with the commutator on the target (in which case it is therefore a
Lie algebra homomorphism).
For G a Lie group and g := Lie(G), we define the adjoint representation

AdG : G →GL(g)

g �→dcg(e)

for

cg : G →G

x �→gxg−1.

To justify the “representation” terminology, let’s check that AdG is a homomorphism:

Lemma 4.15. AdG is a C∞homomorphism.

Proof. Since cg′cg = cg′g, applying the Chain Rule to this at e yields the homomorphism
property. The C∞property comes down to a calculation in local coordinates near the
identity, and is discussed in Appendix C
□

In Appendix C it is shown via elementary calculations with velocity vectors that for any
g ∈GLn(R) we have
AdGLn(R)(g) : M �→gMg−1

for M ∈Matn(R), and similarly for GLn(C) and GLn(H). We are going to see later that for
connected Lie groups, understanding the effect of a Lie group homomorphism f between
tangent spaces at the identity will exert tremendous control on f. But even without
connectedness hypotheses, it is natural to try to understand the induced map

d(AdG)(e) : g →T1 (GL(g)) = EndR(g).

We have already seen another such map, namely adg, and we next want to discuss some
ideas underlying the proof of the following very important result:

Theorem 4.16. For any Lie group G, d(AdG)(e) = adg as maps g →EndR(g).

This result is proved Appendix C, and a key ingredient in its proof as well as in the
proof of the functoriality of [·, ·]G in G is an entirely different description of [·, ·]G using the
notion of “1-parameter subgroup” of G: a homomorphism of Lie groups

α : R →G

which may not be injective (but still called “1-parameter subgroup”!).
Using ODE’s and some differential geometry (as we’ll see next time), one shows:


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
23

Theorem 4.17. For all v ∈g, there exists a unique 1-parameter subgroup αv : R →G so that
α′
v(0) = v.

Example 4.18. If G = GLn(R), then (using uniqueness) αv(t) = etv. The same holds for
GLn(C) and GLn(H).

Example 4.19. If G = S1 × S1 = (R × R)/(Z × Z) then αv is a “wrapped line” on the
torus.

In Appendix C, 1-parameter subgroups αv are used to formulate the following totally
different description of the bracket that is proved there:

[v, w]G = d

dt|t=0 AdG (αv(t)) (w).

In concrete terms, one can think of t �→AdG(αv(t))(w) as a parametric curve in the finite-
dimensional R-vector space g, and then we form its velocity at t = 0. This description of
the bracket is very “local near e ∈G”, in contrast with the initial definition using rather
global notions such as �v and �w.
Next time we will explain how to use this “local” formula for the bracket to deduce the
functoriality of [·, ·]G in G and more importantly we will discuss some of the background
in ODE’s and differential geometry which underlies the proof of Theorem 4.17.

5. MAXIMAL COMPACT AND 1-PARAMETER SUBGROUPS

Today, we’ll discuss 1-parameter subgroups, which were breifly introduced at the end of
last class. But first, to have some sense of how all of our effort on compact Lie groups in this
course will not be time wasted relative to what may be learned later in the non-compact
case, we shall briefly discuss the remarkable sense in which the compact case plays an
essential role in the structure of general Lie groups.
Weyl was the first to carry out a deep general study of the representation theory of
Lie groups, focusing on the compact case (where one has techniques such as integration
without convergence issues). It is truly amazing that the results in the compact case are
important in the non-compact case. To understand this, we mention (without proof) the
following general theorem that is good to be aware of before one puts in a lot of effort to
learn the structure theory in the compact case.

Theorem 5.1. Let G be a Lie group with finitely many connected components.

(i) Every compact subgroup of G is contained in a maximal compact subgroup; i.e., one not
contained in a strictly bigger one.
(ii) All maximal compact subgroups K ⊂G are conjugate, they meet every connected compo-
nent, and K ∩G0 is connected and is a maximal compact subgroup of G0.
(iii) The inclusion of K into G extends to a diffeomorphism of C∞manifolds G ≃K × V for V
a finite dimenaional R-vector space (so K �→G is a deformation retract, and hence G has
the same “homotopy type” as K).

Note that in (i) the first assertion is not a consequence of dimension reasons since it isn’t
evident at the outset why there couldn’t be a strictly rising chain of compact subgroups
that merely become increasingly disconnected without any increase in dimension. Also,
the compact subgroups are necessarily smooth submanifolds (hence “Lie subgroups”,
a notion we’ll discuss in general a bit later) due to an important theorem of Lie to be


24
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

discussed in a few lectures from now: any closed subgroup of a Lie group is a smooth
submanifold.
Theorem 5.1 is proved in [Hoch, Ch. XV, Thm. 3.1]. It is the only reference I know that
allows the possibility of (finitely) disconnected G, which is important for applications to
the group of R-points of an affine group variety over R. I am not aware of any way to
deduce the full result from the connected case treated as a black box (e.g., why should
there exist a maximal K meeting every connected component?); if you can come up with
such a proof, let me know immediately!

Example 5.2. For G = GLn(R), we can take K to be O(n) and

K × Symn(R) ≃G

(k, T) �→keT

(where Symn(R) is the space of symmetric n × n matrices over R). This is called the polar
decomposition.

Example 5.3. For G = O(r, s) we can take K to be O(r) × O(s) via an orthogonal decom-
position of the quadratic space into an r-dimensional positive-definite space and an s-
dimensional negative-definite space. (Note that for any non-degenerate finite-dimensional
quadratic space (V, q) over R we have O(q) = O(−q), so the negative-definite case has its
orthogonal group identified with O(s).)

Example 5.4. If G = U(r, s) then we can take K to be U(r) × U(s).

The three preceding examples are proved in Appendix D: the first is proved by an
elementary averaging argument, and the other two are deduced from that via the spectral
theorem.
Our main focus for today is to discuss the following fundamental result:

Theorem 5.5. Let G be a Lie group and choose v ∈g. There exists a unique 1-parameter subgroup

αv : G →R

such that α′
v(0) = v.

Example 5.6. If G = GLn(F) for F = R, C, H we have αM(t) = etM for M ∈Matn(F).

Many G of interest to us will be a closed subgroup of one of the groups as in the
preceding example, so this computes αv in such cases due to the functoriality of 1-parameter
subgroups:

Lemma 5.7. For f : G →G′ a map of Lie groups, suppose the induced map L f : g →g′ satisfies
v �→v′. Then

f ◦αv = αv′.

Proof. By uniqueness, we just have to compute the velocity at t = 0, which we do via the
Chain Rule:
( f ◦αv)′(0) = (L f )(α′
v(0)) = (L f )(v) =: v′.
□

Remark 5.8. Before we dive into the proof of Theorem 5.5, as motivation for the effort
required we note that Appendix C uses 1-parameter subroups αv to prove two important
results:

5.1. Proof of existence in Theorem 5.5. We’ll now discuss the proof of existence of αv; the
proof of uniqueness will be much easier, and is deferred to next time.
The key input is the theory of integral curves to C∞vector fields on manifolds. The
local content amounts to suitable existence and uniqueness results for first order (possibly
non-linear) ODE’s, as we shall recall below.
We begin with a review of integral curves. Let M be a C∞manifold. Let X be a C∞

vector field on M, and p ∈M a point.


26
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Definition 5.10. An integral curve to X on M at p ∈M is a C∞map

c : I →M

where I is an open interval in R containing 0 such that

• c(0) = p
• c′(t) = X(c(t)) for all t ∈I (so c′(0) = v0 := X(p)).

We emphasize that c is a parametric curve, not a bare 1-dimensional submanifold of
M (as c may well not be injective either, and even if injective it may not be a topological
embedding: think of a densely wrapped line in a 2-dimensional torus). Note that we are
specifying an actual velocity vector in Tc(t)(M) for each t and not merely the “tangent line”
inside Tc(t)(M) for each t.

Example 5.11. If M = R2 −{0} and X = r∂θ then the integral curves are constant-speed
parametric circles around the origin. The constant speed of each parametric circle decreases
as one approaches the origin due to the factor of r in the vector field. Using instead X = ∂θ,
all of these circles would be parametrized at the same speed.

Using local coordinates {y1, . . . , yn} near p, the existence of such c on some short interval
I becomes a first order ODE result, as follows. Consider U ⊂Rn an open subset containing
0 (corresponding to p in the coordinate domain) and a C∞map f : U →Rn (in the case of
interest this encodes X|U = ∑j fj ∂

∂yj). Then we seek a smooth map φ : I →U ⊂Rn so that

• φ(0) = 0,
• φ′(t) = f (φ(t)) for all t ∈I (so φ′(0) = f (0) ∈Rn).
The existence of such a φ is a special case of the usual existence and uniqueness theorem
for first order ODE’s (where the ODE may be have the rather more general form φ′(t) =
f (t, φ(t)) for some f : I × U →Rn rather than the more special “autonomous” form that
arises above).
So the vector-valued 1st-order ODE theory on open subsets of Rn gives existence on
(−ε, ε) for some ε > 0, and gives uniqueness whenever the integral curve valued in U
exists (i.e., for any I on which such an integral curve exists, subject to the integral curve
remaining inside the coordinate domain). For a complete treatement of this local ODE
existence and uniqueness result on open subsets of Rn, see [Te, §2.4] or Appendices E and
F.
We need to push this result inside Rn to the manifold setting, where the integral curve
may not lie entirely inside a coordinate chart around p. Using compactness and connected-
ness inside the real line, it is explained in Appendix F how to build on the serious work in
Rn to get the following more global version:

(1) There exists a unique maximal integral curve cmax
p
: I(p) →M to X at p (i.e., one
defined on an interval I(p) containing all others).
(2) The set

Ω:= {(t, q) ∈R × M : t ∈I(p)}

is open in R × M and the map

Φ: Ω→M
(t, p) �→cmax
p
(t) =: Φt(p)


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
27

is smooth. (This smoothness is very important in what follows, and it requires
much more input from the local ODE theory, also proved in the same references
given above: smooth dependence on initial conditions, which shouldn’t be taken
for granted at all.)

Further, the uniqueness of maximal integral curves yields the following bonus fact (as
discussed in Appendix F):

Fact 5.12. Suppose t ∈I(p) and t′ ∈I (Φt(p)) . Then, t + t′ ∈I(p) and

Φt′+t(p) = Φt′ (Φt(p)) .

Beware that for general (M, X, p), the flow can end in finite time; i.e., we may have
I(p) ̸= R. For example, this happens in R2 −{0} with X = −∂

∂r (for which the integral
curves are constant-speed rays going towards the origin, which all cease to exist in finite
time). But in the Lie group setting with M = G and X = �v a left-invariant vector field,
the homogeneity buys us much better behavior. For example, since “dℓg(X) = X”, so the
entire situation is equivariant for left multiplication by any g ∈G, we have I(gp) = I(p)
for all g ∈G, p ∈G. Hence, by transitivity of the left translation action of G on itself we
get I(p) = I(e) =: I for all p ∈G. Ah, but then Fact 5.12 tells us that the open interval
I ⊂R containing 0 is stable under addition, so necessarily I = R! Thus, we have a “global
flow”

Φ : R × G →G

which satisfies Φt′+t(e) = Φt′(Φt(e)) for all t, t′ ∈R.
Homogeneity under left multiplication also gives Φt(gp) = gΦt(p) for all g, p ∈G, so
Φt(g) = gΦt(e). Thus,

Φt′+t(e) = Φt′(Φt(e)) = Φt(e)Φt′(e).

The upshot of this discussion is that t �→Φt(e) is a C∞homomorphism R →G, and being
an integral curve to X = �v implies that its velocity at t = 0 is X(e) = v. We have thereby
proved the existence of αv (namely, define αv(t) = Φt(e): this is flow to time t along the
integral curve through e at time 0 for the vector field X = �v).

6. THE EXPONENTIAL MAP

Remark 6.1. For us, all manifolds are second countable so we will be able to apply Sard’s
theorem.

Last time, we saw existence of 1-parameter subgroups. Today, we’ll begin by showing
uniqueness of 1-parameter subgroups:

Proposition 6.2. The 1-parameter subgroup α : R →G with α′(0) = v ∈g is unique.

Proof. The idea is to use the uniqueness assertion for integral curves (which we saw
essentially boils down to the uniqueness theorem for 1st-order ODE’s).
For fixed t, define the parametric curve in G:

α(t + τ) := α(t)α(τ) = ℓα(t)α(τ).


28
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Since ℓα(t) is a C∞map having nothing to do with τ, by viewing both sides as parametric
curves with parameter τ and applying d

dτ (i.e., computing velocity vectors on both sides)
we get

α′(t + τ) = dℓα(t)(α(τ))(α′(τ))

Setting τ = 0, we find

α′(t) = dℓα(t)(e)

= dℓα(t)(e) (v)

= �v (α(t)) .

This implies that α is an integral curve to �v with α(0) = e ∈G, and it is defined on all of R.
Now use the uniqueness result for integral curves on the maximal interval of definition to
conclude that α is unique.
□

Definition 6.3. The exponential map expG : g →G is defined to be v �→αv(1), where αv is
the 1-parameter subgroup associated to v ∈g.

Example 6.4. Take G = GLn(F) with F = R, C, H, so g = Matn(F). We then obtain
αM(t) = etM, so αM(1) = eM (explaining the “exponential map” name). In particular, we
see expG need not be a homomorphism, and typically won’t be.

Lemma 6.5. The exponential map satisfies the following properties:

(1) expG(0) = e;
(2) For all t ∈R and v ∈g, we have expG(tv) := αtv(1) = αv(t).
(3) The map expG is functorial in G; i.e., for any map of Lie groups f : G →H the diagram

(6.1)

g
h

G
H

expG

L f

expH
f

commutes.

Combining the functoriality with Example 6.4 allows us to calculate expG for many G of
interest (which arise as a closed subgroup of some GLn(F) with F = R, C, H).

Proof. We prove the statements in order.

(1) This follows from the definition, since the unique 1-parameter subgroup associated
to v = 0 is the trivial homomorphism.
(2) For fixed t, αtv(s) and αv(ts) are 1-parameter subgroups in s with the same veloc-
ity at s = 0: they both have velocity tv. Hence, by uniqueness of 1-parameter
subgroups, they must be equal. Then set s = 1 to conclude.
(3) This holds because αv is functorial in (G, v), as we noted last time.
□

Remark 6.6. It is a natural to expect, especially from the explicit examples with GLn(F) as
above (and so for closed subgroups thereof), that the map of manifolds expG : g →G is C∞.
This is not an obvious consequence of “smooth dependence on initial conditions” since the


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
29

definition of αv is in terms of the global vector field �v, making the smooth behavior under
variation of v not immediately apparent.
The smoothness of expG for general G is a very important technical point which we will
use all over the place in what follows. For a proof, we refer to Appendix G.

Lemma 6.7. The map

d(expG)(0) : g ≃T0(g) →Te(G) =: g

is the identity map, where the first step is an instance of the canonical isomorphism of a finite-
dimensional R-vector space with its tangent space at any point (via directional derivatives).
In particular (by the inverse function theorem), expG is a diffeomorphism between some open
neighborhoods of 0 ∈g and e ∈G.

Proof. We saw above that expG(tv) = αv(t) as parameteric curves. Computing the velocity
at any t, we find

((d expG)(tv))(v) = α′
v(t).

Setting t = 0, we get ((d expG)(0))(v) = α′
v(0) = v. To conclude, the careful reader
should check that the identifications we made in the statement of the lemma agree with
the identifications made above in this proof.
□

Corollary 6.8. If f : G →G′ is a map of Lie groups and G is connected then f is uniquely
determined by L f : g →g′.

Proof. By functoriality of exp, we have a commutative diagram

(6.2)

g
g′

G
G′.

L f

expG
expG′

f

Since expG is a diffeomorphism between open neighborhoods of 0 ∈g and e ∈G, given L f
we know f on some open U ∋e in G. (In fact, this open U is independent of f, depending
only on G, but we don’t need that.) Thus, the homomorphism f is determined on the open
subgroup H ⊂G generated by U (here, H consists of all “words” in products of finitely
many elements of U and their inverses, the openness of this set of words being an easy
exercise). But open subgroups are always closed in any topological group because their
nontrivial cosets are each open, so H is also closed in G. Since G is connected, we conclude
that H = G, so f is uniquely determined.
□

Note that connectedness of G cannot be dropped in Corollary 6.8, since L f clearly exerts
no control on what is happening in non-identity components of G (e.g., consider the case
when G is a non-trivial discrete group, such as a non-trivial finite group, so g = 0).

Lemma 6.9. If G is commutative then [·, ·]G = 0.

Proof. This follows from the Local Formula for [·, ·] since AdG is identically equal to e by
commutativity.
□

We have a converse in the connected case, via two applications of Corollary 6.8:

Corollary 6.10. If G is connected and [·, ·]G = 0 then G is commutative.


30
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Clearly the connectedness cannot be dropped here (much as in Corollary 6.8).

Proof. We have Lie(AdG) = adg = 0, so by Corollary 6.8 applied to f = AdG necessarily

AdG : G →GL(g)
g �→idg

This says d(cg)(e) = idg for all g ∈G, so cg = idG for all g ∈G (by Corollary 6.8 applied
to f = cg). But that triviality for all g says exactly that G is commutative.
□

6.1. Classification of connected commutative Lie groups. Let’s now describe all con-
nected commutative Lie groups.

Lemma 6.11. For a connected commutative G,

expG : g →G

is a surjective homomorphism with discrete kernel.

Proof. To start, let’s see why expG is a homomorphism. Commutative G have the following
disorienting feature:

m : G × G →G

is a homomorphism (where the source is viewed as a direct product of Lie groups).
Using functoriality of expG, and that expG×G = expG × expG (i.e., exp behaves well for
direct product of Lie groups, which is obvious due to the same for integral curves or for a
zillion other reasons), we get a commutative diagram

(6.3)

g × g
g

G × G
G

L(m)

expG × expG
expG

m

Since L(m) is vector addition (as for any Lie group whatsoever), this commutative diagram
says exactly that expG is a homomorphism. Furthermore, the subset expG(g) ⊂G contains
an open around e since expG is a diffeomorphism near 0.
But expG(g) is also a subgroup of G since expG is a homomorphism, so expG(g) is an
open subgroup of G. We have already noted that any open subgroup is always closed, so
since G is connected we conclude that expG(g) = G, which says that expG is surjective.
Finally, to show the kernel is discrete, simply use that expG is a local diffeomorphism
near 0. This provides some open neighborhood U of 0 in g so that ker(expG) ∩U = {0},
which gives discreteness of the kernel.
□

To further develop our understanding of connected commutative Lie groups, we need
to know the discrete subgroups of g ≃Rn. The following basic result is [BtD, Lemma 3.8,
Ch. I] (and we urge a reader who hasn’t encountered it before to try to prove the cases
n ≤2 on their own, to appreciate the content):

Fact 6.12. Any discrete subgroup Γ ⊂Rn is the Z-span of part of a R-basis.


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
31

For G any connected commutative Lie group, we conclude via translation considerations
that the surjective homomorphism expG : g →G is a local diffeomorphism everywhere
due to its behavior as such near 0 ∈g. Hence, even without needing a fancy general theory
of C∞-structures on coset spaces for Lie groups modulo closed Lie subgroups (which we’ll
come to shortly), we obtain a canonical isomorphism of Lie groups

G ≃g/Λ

where Λ ⊂g ≃Rn is the Z-span of part of an R-basis. Since R/Z = S1, we obtain:

Theorem 6.13. The connected commutative Lie groups are precisely the groups (S1)r × Rm. In
particular, the compact connected commutative Lie groups are precisely the groups (S1)r.

Any group of the form (S1)r is called a torus, due to the picture when r = 2.

Remark 6.14. A nice application given in Appendix G is to use the preceding theorem to
give a remarkable proof (due to Witt, and rediscovered by B. Gross when he was a graduate
student) of the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra (using that if m > 2 then Rm −{0} is
simply connected). This is not circular because nowhere in our development of Lie theory
up to here have we used the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra.

6.2. Three applications of the exponential map.

Theorem 6.15 (Closed Subgroup Theorem (E. Cartan)). If H ⊂G is a subgroup of a Lie group,
then H is closed in G if and only if H is a locally closed smooth submanifold.

Proof. See [BtD, Theorem 3.11, Ch. I] for a full proof. We now explain the basic idea for
the harder implication: if H is closed then it is a C∞submanifold. The idea is to cook up
a subspace h ⊂g that is a candidate for Lie(H). One can then use closedness to show
that for a suitably small open U ⊂h around 0, the subset expG(U) ⊂G is open in H.
Then, expG(U) gives a smooth chart on H near e which is translated around to define a
C∞-structure on H that is seen to do the job.
□

As an application of the Closed Subgroup Theorem, we obtain:

Theorem 6.16. Any continuous homomorphism f : G →G′ between Lie groups is C∞.

Proof. For a proof, see Appendix G. The key idea is to consider the graph Γ f ⊂G × G′.
This is a closed subgroup of the Lie group G × G′ because f is a homomorphism, so by
Theorem 6.15 it is even a C∞-submanifold.
Thus, the two maps

(6.4)

Γ f

G
G′

π1
π2

are C∞, and the first one is a bijection. Although a C∞bijection between smooth manifolds
is generally not a diffeomorphism (think of x3 on R), Sard’s theorem ensures that there
are lots of points where such a map is a submersion. The homogeneity then ensures that
for a homomorphism of Lie groups the submersion property holds everywhere, and from
this one can conclude that π1 is a diffeomorphism. Hence, π2 ◦π−1
1
: G →G′ is a C∞map,
and this is f by another name!
□

BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Theorem 6.17. For H ⊂G a closed subgroup, there exists a unique C∞-structure on G/H so
that

π : G →G/H

is a submersion making the natural map g →Te(G/H) induce an isomorphism

g/h ≃Te(G/H).

Moreover, this is initial among all C∞maps of manifolds G →M that are invariant under right
multiplication by H on G.

Proof. A reference for all but the final assertion is [BtD, pp. 34-35] (and the final assertion
is Exercise 5(i) in HW3). The idea for the existence of the asserted C∞-structure on G/H
is to choose a linear complement V to h in g and show that expG applied to a small open
around 0 in V provides the required C∞-structure on G/H near the identity coset e. This
C∞-structure near e is translated around using left multiplication on G/H by elements of
G (and is shown to work and be unique making π a submersion with the specified tangent
space at e).
□

Remark 6.18. It is a remarkable fact that every Cr Lie group with 1 ≤r ≤∞admits a
real-analytic structure making its group law and inversion both real-analytic, and this is
unique. More specifically, for 1 ≤r ≤∞the forgetful functor from real-analytic Lie groups
to Cr Lie groups is an equivalence of categories.
The essential issue is to reconstruct the group law from the Lie bracket via a universal
recipe called the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula: one shows for X, Y ∈g near 0 that
expG(X) expG(Y) = expG(Z(X, Y)) for a “universal formal series Z(X, Y) in iterated
applications of [·, ·]” that has nothing to do with G (but of course for specific G some of
the brackets in this universal formula might happen to vanish). For the definition of the
BCH formula as a “formal Lie series” see [Se, Part I, §4.8] or [Bou1, Ch. II, §6.4], and for its
convergence near (0, 0) see [Se, Part II, §5.4] or [Bou1, Ch. II, §7.2, Prop. 1].
We will never need the real-analytic aspects of Lie groups, but they are very useful in
the general representation theory of non-compact Lie groups (whereas in the compact case
there is a much stronger feature than unique real-analyticity: a “unique algebraicity” which
we will formulate later in the course). If you’re interested to learn about the real-analytic
enhancement as discussed above then read [Se, Part II] and [Bou1, Ch. III, §1.9, Prop. 18].

7. SUBGROUP-SUBALGEBRA CORRESPONDENCE

As an application of expG and differential geometry, for a Lie group G we want to relate
Lie subalgebras of g and connected “Lie subgroups” of G.

Definition 7.1. Let G be a Lie group. A Lie subgroup of G is a pair (H, j) for a Lie group H
and an injective immersion j : H →G.

Example 7.2. The 1-parameter subgroup R →S1 × S1 = (R × R)/(Z × Z) corresponding
to a tangent vector in R × R with slope not in Q is a Lie subgroup that is not a topological
embedding.

Remark 7.3. In Appendix H (whose core geometric input is done in [Wa]), we develop a
higher-dimensional analogue of integral curves to vector fields: given a rank-d subbundle
E ⊂TM (this means a specification of d-dimensional subspaces E(m) ⊂Tm(M) that “vary


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
33

smoothly” in m ∈M), an integral manifold in M to E is an injective immersion N
j−→M
inducing Tn(N) ≃E(j(n)) ⊂Tj(n)(M) for all n ∈N.
Note that for d = 1, such an E is less information than a non-vanishing smooth vector
field because we are only giving a (smoothly varying) line in a tangent space at each point
of M rather than a (smoothly varying) nonzero vector in the tangent space at each point of
M. Also for d = 1 an integral manifold is like an integral curve to a smooth vector field
except that we drop the parameterization and only keep track of the image. For example,
the integral curve to a non-vanishing smooth vector field could be a map from all of R onto
a circle whereas an integral submanifold to the associated line subbundle of TM would
such a circle without a specified parameterization.

The existence of an integral submanifold N to E through each m0 ∈M requires the
vanishing of certain PDE obstructions (which always vanish when d = 1 but are typically
non-trivial when d > 1). The global Frobenius theorem says that this necessary vanishing
condition is also sufficient. When M is a Lie group G and E arises from a Lie subalgebra h
of g (in a manner explained in Appendix H), the stability of h under the Lie bracket on g
implies that the PDE obstructions vanish. That enables one to use the global Frobenius
theorem to prove the surjectivity in the following important result which revolutionized
the theory of general Lie groups (by connecting it tightly to global differential geometry in
a way that had not previously been done):

Theorem 7.4 (Chevalley). The natural map

(7.1)
{connected Lie subgroups H ⊂G} →{Lie subalgebras h ⊂g}

is a bijection. Also, for a connected Lie group G′, a homomorphism f : G′ →G factors in the C∞

sense through a connected Lie subgroup H �→G if and only if L f : g′ →g lands in h ⊂g.

Warning 7.5.

�
Note that H →G in Theorem 7.4 is usually not an embedding (equiva-
lently, H may not be closed in G)! If one only wanted to get a result for closed H for general
G then the proof would be hopeless because it is generally impossible to detect in terms of
h whether or not H is closed in G (though there are very useful sufficient conditions for
this in terms of the structure theory of Lie algebras, with a proof requiring input from the
algebro-geometric theory of linear algebraic groups, so lying rather beyond the level of
this course).

The proof of Theorem 7.4 is given as Theorem H.4.3 and Proposition H.4.4. Injectivity of
the map (7.1) when limited to closed H’s is an easy application of exponential maps.
Here is an important consequence that is good to be aware of but which we will never
use in this course (and which was known to Lie only in a very localized form in the
language of convergent power series and PDE’s):

Corollary 7.6 (Lie’s 3rd Fundamental Theorem). Every finite dimensional Lie algebra h over R
is isomorphic to Lie(H) for some connected Lie group H.

Proof. Apply Theorem 7.4 to G = GLn(R) for any gln(R) containing h as a Lie subalgebra
(provided by Ado’s Theorem applied to h).
□

See §H.4 for many more applications of Theorem 7.4. For example, there are applica-
tions to computing the Lie subalgebras in g corresponding to normalizers, centralizers,


34
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

intersections, and preimages (under homomorphisms) of closed (sometimes connected)
subgroups of connected Lie groups.

7.1. Applications in representation theory. Let’s consider representations of Lie groups
G on finite-dimensional C-vector spaces V. The main case for this course will be compact
G, but for now we allow general G. Suppose we have a C∞(or equivalently, continuous)
map

ρ : G →GL(V) = GLn(C).

In the general compact case, we can use “volume-1 Haar measure” to get results akin to
the case G is finite, where one uses the averaging operator

1
#G ∑
g∈G

to prove results such as complete reducibility of representations. Next time this Haar-
measure method will be discussed for compact G. But what about the non-compact case,
for which such an averaging operator doesn’t exist?
Is there a way to prove complete reducibility results when G is non-compact? We now
explain a technique for achieving this in certain situations when G is connected by using
Theorem 7.4. (Note that for finite G, connectedness forces G = 1. Hence, work with
connected G tells us nothing interesting about the case of finite groups.)
For connected G, note that ρ : G →GL(V) is determined by the map

Lρ : g →gl(V),

which is in turn equivalent to the map

gC := g ⊗R C →gl(V)

of Lie algebras over C. In turn, this map of complex Lie algebras is equivalent to a map

(7.2)
gC × V →V

(denoted (X, v) �→X.v) which is C-bilinear and satisfies

[X, Y] .v = X. (Y.v) −Y. (X.v)

(such a pairing is called a representation of the Lie algebra gC on V).

Lemma 7.7 (Weyl). Assume G is connected. A C-subspace V′ ⊂V is G-stable if and only if it is
gC-stable under the corresponding gC-representation on V in the sense of (7.2). In particular, V
is completely reducible for G (i.e., a direct sum of irreducible representations) if and only if it is
completely reducible for gC.

Proof. Choose a C-basis for V extending one for V′, with d = dim V′ and n = dim V. Then
V′ is G-stable if and only if ρ : G →GL(V) = GLn(C) lands in the closed Lie subgroup H
of invertible n × n matrices with vanishing lower-right (n −d) × d block (as that expresses
the preservation of V′ for a linear automorphism of V). Since G and H are connected, this
is equivalent to the map Lρ : g →gl(V) = gln(C) landing in Lie(H) by Theorem 7.4!
But Lie(H) consists of those elements of gl(V) = Matn(C) with vanishing lower-right
(n −d) × d block, which in turn corresponds to the elements of gl(V) = End(V) which
carry V′ into itself, so this is equivalent to the condition gC · V′ ⊂V′.
□


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
35

Example 7.8 (Weyl’s unitary trick). Suppose gC = g′
C for g′ = Lie(G′) with a connected
compact Lie group G′. (We’ll see later that this is a very mild hypothesis on g, satisfied
in many interesting cases with non-compact G.) Furthermore assume that the resulting
representation

g′ →g′
C = gC
(Lρ)C
−−−→gl(V)

arises from a Lie group representation

ρ′ : G′ →GL(V).

(We say the Lie algebra map “integrates” to ρ′, since passage from Lie groups to Lie
algebras is a kind of “derivative” process.) The existence of ρ′ has some topological
obstructions in general, but later we will see that such obstructions can often be bypassed
by adjusting G′ slightly, and in particular are never an issue when G′ is simply connected.
By Lemma 7.7, the complete reducibility for (G, ρ) is equivalent to that for (G′, ρ′), which
holds in turn (by averaging arguments we’ll discuss next time) because G′ is compact!
This is like an out-of-body experience, since:

(i) neither G nor G′ live inside the other, so they don’t “directly communicate” as Lie
groups,
(ii) the contact between G and G′ is entirely through their complexified Lie algebras,
which aren’t visibly the Lie algebras of any complex Lie groups easily attached to
G or G′ (e.g., G may not have anything to do with algebraic geometry)!

As an example, for G = SLn(R) we have sln(R)C = sln(C) = su(n)C (we’ll see the sec-
ond equality later) and G′ := SU(n) is simply connected (to be proved later). More broadly,
it is a general fact that every semisimple Lie algebra over R (of which the sln(R)’s are a
special case) has complexification that coincides with the complexification of a (unique)
connected compact Lie group that is simply connected. Hence, complete reducibility for
representations of connected Lie groups with a semisimple Lie algebra (which constitute a
large and important class of Lie groups) can be reduced to the known case of (connected)
compact Lie groups! This is called the “unitary trick” since any continuous representation
ρ of a compact Lie group K into GLn(C) leaves invariant some positive-definite hermitian
form on Cn (by averaging on the compact group ρ(K)) and hence lands in U(n) after a
change of basis.

Warning 7.9.

It may be that ρ′ as in Example 7.8 does not exist if G′ is chosen poorly.

Since 1 is the only compact subgroup of R>0, we obtain the important:

Theorem 8.9. For G a compact Lie group we have ∆G = 1, so the left Haar measures µω are
bi-invariant. If G is also connected then even the left-invariant top-degree differential forms ω are
bi-invariant.

Warning 8.10.

�
It can happen for G compact but disconnected that although µω (and so
really the “density” |ω|) is bi-invariant, the differential form ω is not bi-invawriant. An
example for which this happens is G = O(2) = SO(2) ⋊Z/2Z, for which the non-trivial
element of Z/2Z conjugates on the circle SO(2) via inversion (which has the effect of
negation on the invariant 1-form dt/t on the circle).
For some other disconnected compact groups, such as SO(r, s) with r, s > 0, the bi-
invariance does hold at the level of differential forms. For those who know a bit about
schemes: the secret conceptual distinction between the disconnected groups SO(r, s) and
O(2) (or more generally O(n) for n > 1) that explains this difference in bi-invariance at the

level of top-degree left-invariant differential forms is that SO(r, s) is “Zariski-connected”
whereas O(n) is never “Zariski-connected”.

Remark 8.12. For compact (perhaps disconnected!) G there is a canonical Haar measure
dg characterized by
�

G dg = 1.

(Indeed, by regularity of the measure and compactness of G any Haar measure µ satisfies
0 < �

G dµ < ∞, so we can uniquely scale µ by R>0 to arrange the total volume to be 1.)
For finite G, this corresponds to the point mass 1/(#G) at all g ∈G, which gives rise to the
averaging procedure used a lot in the representation theory of finite groups, such as to
make a G-invariant positive-definite hermitian form on any (finite-dimensional, C-linear!)
G-representation V. Such a hermitian form h ensures that any G-subrepresentation W ⊂V
has a G-stable linear complement, namely its h-orthogonal complement W⊥⊂V. This
yields complete reducibility for representations of finite groups G by dimension induction.

To establish complete reducibility for compact Lie groups by the exact same argument
as for finite groups, we just need:

Proposition 8.13. For compact Lie groups G and (continuous finite-dimensional!) representation

ρ : G →GL(V),

there exists a G-invariant positive-definite hermitian form on V.

Proof. In Appendix D we use �

ρ(G) to show ρ(G) ⊂U(h) for some positive-definite
hermitian h on V. Such an inclusion is precisely the statement that G leaves h invariant:

h(gv, gv′) = h(v, v′) for all v, v′ ∈V, g ∈G.

□

Remark 8.14. Positive-definiteness of the hermitian form (rather than non-degeneracy) is
crucial to ensure that when we restrict to subspaces it remains nondegenerate and that
orthogonal complements are linear complements. More importantly, in the averaging
process used to construct the invariant h from an initial choice of h0 on V, if h0 were merely


40
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

non-degenerate rather than positive-definite then we would have no reason to expect that
the G-averaged h obtained from h0 is even non-degenerate.

Lemma 8.16 (Schur’s lemma). For an irreducible finite-dimensional C-linear representation
ρ : Γ →GL(V) of an arbitrary group Γ,

EndΓ(V) = C.

dimC HomΓ(V, W) =

Proof. One uses the same eigenvalue argument (working over C) as for finite groups. This
is pure algebra, requiring nothing about the group Γ (no topology, etc.).
□

Corollary 8.17. For a commutative topological group G and (continuous finite-dimensional C-
linear) irreducible representation ρ : G →GL(V), necessarily dim V = 1.
That is, any such (V, ρ) corresponds to a continuous character χ : G →C×.

Proof. Since G is commutative, G acts on V through EndG(V), and this in turn consists of
the scalar endomorphisms by Schur’s Lemma. Therefore, G acts through EndG(V)× = C×,
which is to say ρ(g) is a scalar for all g ∈G. Thus, all subspaces of V are G-stable but ρ is
irreducible, so V must be 1-dimensional.
□

Corollary 8.18. For a compact commutative Lie groups G, every representation is a direct sum of
1-dimensional representations.

Proof. This follows from complete reducibility and Corollary 8.17.
□

Here is an example that will be extremely important for the rest of the course.

9. CHARACTER THEORY

9.1. Isotypic decomposition. Recall that for finite groups, the decomposition of a repre-
sentation (always finite-dimensional, C-linear) as a direct sum of irreducible representa-
tions is generally not unique in any intrinsic/functorial sense, but the multiplicity with
which each irreducible appears in such a decomposition is intrinsic. Similarly, for any rep-
resentation V of a compact Lie group G, the decomposition into irreducibles is not intrinsic.
However, by exactly the same argument as for finite groups, the isotypic decomposition

V =
�

σ irreducible
V(σ)

for the span V(σ) of all copies of σ inside V is both intrinsic and functorial. A slicker
description of V(σ) is this:

V(σ) = image(σ ⊗C HomG(σ, V) →V)

(G-equivariant map via evaluation, injective by Schur’s Lemma).

Definition 9.1. For V a representation of a compact Lie group G and σ an irreducible
representation of G, HomG(σ, V) is called the multiplicity space. (The reason for the name
is that by Schur’s lemma, dim HomG(σ, V) is the multiplicity of σ in a decomposition of V
as a direct sum of irreducible representations.)


42
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Definition 9.2. For a torus T, the characters χ : T →C× with V(χ) ̸= 0 are called the
weights of V. For general χ, we call V (χ) the χ-weight space of V (it might vanish).

By functoriality of the isotypic decomposition, for a subrepresentation W ⊂V we
have W = ⊕W(σ) with W(σ) = W ∩V(σ) ⊂V(σ). Nothing like this is available in the
language of the decomposition as a direct sum of irreducible representations; this is why
the isotypic decomposition is sometimes more useful than mere complete reducibility in
constructions and proofs.

9.2. Application of the weight space decomposition for tori. We’ll next describe the
irreducible representations of SO(3). The natural quotient map

SU(2) →SO(3)

has central kernel {±1}, so the irreducible representations of SO(3) are those irreducible
representations of SU(2) on which −1 acts trivially. Note that on any irreducible represen-
tation of SU(2) the action of the central −1 must be via a scalar (by Schur’s lemma), so
since (−1)2 = 1 in SU(2) we see that the scalar must be ±1 ∈C×.
For n ≥0 consider

Vn := Symn(C2)

as a representation of SU(2) ⊂GL2(C), where C2 denotes the representation of SU(2)
arising from its tautological inclusion into GL2(C). Note that dim Vn = n + 1. Concretely,

Vn = {degree-n homogeneous polynomials P(z1, z2)}

with

Lemma 9.3. The Vn’s for which SU(2) factors through SO(3) are the cases of even n.

Proof. By definition, the effect of g = −1 is

P(z) �→P(−z) = (−1)n P(z),

so −1 acts by multiplication by (−1)n. Therefore, the Vn’s for which SU(2) factors through
SO(3) are the cases of even n.

Proposition 9.4. The representations Vn are irreducible for SU(2).

Proof. The rough idea is to compute the weight space decomposition of Vn for some torus
T ⊂SU(2), then do the same for another torus T′ ⊂SU(2), and finally compare them.
This is not the “professional” way of proceeding (which would be to use character theory
that we haven’t yet introduced), but it concretely illustrates the power of working with
maximal tori in connected compact Lie groups G to get a handle on the representation
theory of any such G; this will be an important theme later in the course.

The proof of this uses character theory. We now introduce the basic ideas in character

this to prove the above Theorem is developed in HW4. In all that we do for this topic, the
only role of the “Lie” condition is that it makes the theory of Haar measure more tangible;
granting the existence and uniqueness theorems on Haar measure (and continuity of the
