modulus character), all that we do applies without change to any compact Hausdorff
topological group.

9.3. Characters of compact Lie groups. Let G be a compact Lie group and ρ : G →GL(V)
a representation. (Recall all representations are assumed to be C-linear, finite-dimensional,
and continuous.)

Definition 9.6. The character of (V, ρ) is

χV(g) = χρ(g) := tr (ρ(g)) .

Remark 9.7. This is a class function and is C∞as a map G →C because ρ is C∞(and hence
the matrix entries of ρ are C∞-functions on G). We will never need this; continuity of χV is
entirely sufficient for our needs (to ensure various integrals below make sense without
any complications).

Example 9.8. For V and V′ two G representations, we get the representation V ⊗V′ via

g.

Example 9.9. For a G-representation V, the dual V∗is a representation via

(g · ℓ) (v) = ℓ(ρ(g−1)(v)).

(The use of g−1 ensures we have a left action.) Thus,

χV∗= tr(ρ(g−1)∗) = tr(ρ(g−1)) = tr(ρ(g)),

the last step using that the eigenvalues of ρ(g) lie in S1 (where z−1 = z). To see this,
we use that by compactness of ρ(G) we have ρ(G) ⊂U(h) for some positive-definite
hermitian form h on V (and by definition of the hermitian condition, any eigenvector for
an h-hermitian operator on V has eigenvalues in S1). Alternatively, if the eigenvalue were
not on the unit circle then its powers would become unbounded or approach 0, which
would contradict compactness of ρ(G) ⊂GL(V). Therefore, χV∗= χV.

For two G-representations V and W, make HomC (V, W) a G-representation via

g.T = ρW(g) ◦T ◦ρV(g)−1,

so HomC (V, W)G = HomC[G](V, W). It is an instructive exercise to check (by computing
on elementary tensors) that the natural isomorphism of C-vector spaces

10. WEYL’S UNITARY TRICK

Today we will discuss Weyl’s unitary trick. This is a method to use compact Lie groups to
control the representation theory of non-compact Lie groups via relating their Lie algebras.
As an example, we will be able to use SU(n) to study suitable representations of SLn(C).
Most of what we discuss today is being said just for general cultural awareness of what is
“out there”; none of this will be used later in the course except for the discussion of the
representation theory of sl2(C) which we’ll come to near the end of today. In particular,
apart from this final topic, we will say very little about the details of proofs, just some
highlights.

10.1. Warm-up to complex structure. At the end of §H.4, we have:

Theorem 10.1. If G, G′ are Lie groups with G connected and π1(G) = 1 (i.e., G is simply
connected), then

HomLie(G, G′) ≃HomR- Lie(g, g′).

For example, the verification of the triviality of π1(G) for G = SU(n), Sp(n) is treated in
HW5.

Example 10.2. Take G′ = GLn(F) for F = R or C. Then for G as in Theorem 10.1, a
continuous homomorphism ρ : G →G′ is the “same” as a map of Lie algebras Lρ : g →
g′ = Matn(F) over R. Concretely, the map Lρ is given by

Lρ: g →g′

v �→d

�

��
.

Corollary 10.3. Suppose G is connected and simply connected. Passing to Lie algebras defines a
bijection of sets of isomorphism classes:

(10.1)

{n-dimensional representations of G over C up to isomorphism}

{n-dimensional representations of the real Lie algebra g over C}

Note that n-dimensional representations of the real Lie algebra g can be though of as
suitable R-bilinear pairings g × V →V for an n-dimensional C-vector space V (carrying
the Lie bracket on g over to the commutator of C-linear endomorphisms of V).
This can also be thought of as V being given a structure of left module over the universal
enveloping algebra

U(g) = T(g)/⟨X ⊗Y −Y ⊗X −[X, Y]⟩

where T(W) is the tensor algebra ⊕m≥0W⊗m for a vector space W and we form the quotient
by the 2-sided ideal generated by the indicated relations. In the purely algebraic theory
of Lie algebras, one can say quite a bit about the structure of U(g) when g has favorable
properties (such as being “semisimple” over a field of characteristic 0).
An important refinement of the preceding considerations is to consider G a complex
Lie group and ρ a finite-dimensional holomorphic representation (i.e., ρ : G →GL(V)
is a map of complex Lie groups, a much stronger condition that being a map of C∞real


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
47

manifolds). We can ask: is there a bijection like Theorem 10.1 in the holomorphic setting?
Yes!
The key point is that Frobenius theorems still hold in the complex-analytic setting
without redoing the proofs: the crux is that if M is a complex manifold and N ⊂M is a
closed C∞-submanifold then N is a complex submanifold if and only if for all n ∈N the
R-subspace

Tn(N) ⊂Tn(M)

is a C-subspace. Using this, one can show the following:

Fact 10.4. In the setup of Theorem 10.1 (so G is connected and simply connected) with G
and G′ both complex Lie groups, the natural map

Homhol(G, G′) →HomC- Lie
�
g, g′�

is bijective.

10.2. Algebraicity and holomorphicity. We now take a brief digression to explain a fact
which works over C, but completely fails over R (roughly due to the additional subtleties
of the links between analytic and algebraic geometry over R as opposed to over C). We
first define unipotence. There are many equivalent definitions. Here is one:

Definition 10.5. An n × n matrix over a field is unipotent if its eigenvalues all equal 1.

Miracle 10.6. Suppose G and G′ are respectively Zariski-closed over C in GL(V) and
GL(V′) for finite-dimensional C-vector spaces V, V′ such that G is “reductive”: has no
non-trivial Zariski-closed normal subgroup all of whose elements are unipotent (there are
more elegant ways to think about this which make it look less ad hoc).
The miracle is that all holomorphic homomorphisms G →G′ are necessarily algebraic;
i.e., they are given by everywhere-defined rational functions in the matrix entries. For
example, the reductivity condition holds for G = SLn(C), Sp2n(C) (and many many more
examples which we don’t have time to discuss). This is a result in the spirit of Serre’s
GAGA theorem for projective varieties over C, but it has nothing whatsoever to do with
those results; it is very specific to the “affine group variety” setting over C.

Remark 10.7. The avoidance of unipotent normal subgroups (i.e., the reductivity condition)
in Miracle 10.6 is crucial. For example, it rules out the holomorphic homomorphism

exp: C →C×
�
1
z
0
1

�
�→ez

that is visibly not “algebraic” as a map from the affine line to the affine curve GL1.

Warning 10.8.

�
Beware that Miracle 10.6 fails over R! (The proof of Miracle 10.6 uses
in a crucial way that algebraic geometry over C is in certain senses better-behaved than
algebraic geometry over R.) For example, one can exhibit “PGL3” as an affine group
variety over R yet the inverse to the real-analytic isomorphism SL3(R) →PGL3(R) (the
real-analyticity of cube root on R× has no analogue on the entirety of C×) gives rise to a
real-analytic representation PGL3(R) ≃SL3(R) →GL3(R) that can be shown to not arise
from an “algebraic” map PGL3 →GL3 (in the sense of algebraic geometry over R).

BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

10.3. Using Weyl’s unitary trick to understand representations of SLn(C). By definition
we have SU(n) ⊂SLn(C), so naturally su(n) ⊂sln(C) as Lie algebras over R. As shown
in HW4 in the case n = 2 and will be shown later in general, the induced map

C ⊗R su(n) →sln(C)

of Lie algebras over C is an isomorphism. (Concretely, an R-basis of su(n) is a C-basis of
sln(C).) Therefore, at the level of categories of finite-dimensional C-linear representations,

RepC(SU(n)) = RepC
R- Lie(su(n))

= RepC
C- Lie(sln(C))
= Rephol(SLn(C))

(using that π1(SLn(C)) = 1 and π1(SU(n)) = 1). But SU(n) is compact, so the objects in the
source category are completely reducible, and hence the finite-dimensional holomorphic
representations of SLn(C) are also completely irreducible.
Such representations of SLn(C) can be classified by thinking in terms of the representa-
tion theory of sln(C). The latter task is a special case of the general theory of semisimple
Lie algebras (over a field of characteristic 0), for which the representation theory is best
understood using root systems (a purely combinatorial structure that we will introduce
later as a powerful tool in the structure theory of connected compact Lie groups). Let’s
now focus on understanding the representations of sl2(C), since this will be important in
our subsequent work: remarkably, in the general structure theory of connected compact
Lie groups the good properties of sl2(C) = su(2)C play a crucial role.

Remark 10.11. Note that the eigenspace kv0 for X+|W as in the statement of Theorem 10.10
is 1-dimensional and has H-eigenvalue m strictly large than all others. We call m the highest
H-weight in W, and call v0 a “highest-weight vector” in W.

Remark 10.12. In Appendix J, the above list of all irreducible finite-dimensional sl2(k)-
representations is used to prove (via Ext’s over a universal enveloping algebra) that the
representation theory over k of sl2(k) is completely reducible! We know the complete
reducibility for k = C by topological/analytic means via the unitary trick, but for later
purposes it will be very important that we have found all the irreducible finite-dimensional
representations of sl2(C); this in turn is also established via the unitary trick in HW4
by means of SU(2), but it is interesting that Appendix J gives the same result by purely
algebraic means over any field of characteristic 0.
For our purposes the work in Appendix J should be regarded as an instructive warm-
up in hands-on work with the Lie algebra sl2. In this way one sees that the general
development of the structure theory of compact Lie groups later in the course does not
logically depend on the unitary trick.

11. WEYL’S CONJUGACY THEOREM: APPLICATIONS

We’ll start by stating the Conjugacy Theorem and spend the rest of today deducing some
applications which will have tremendous importance throughout the rest of the course.


50
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

These will also provide a compelling reason to care about the Conjugacy Theorem, whose
proof will require a couple of lectures.

Theorem 11.1 (Weyl’s Conjugacy Theorem). Let G be a connected compact Lie group.

(1) All maximal tori in G (i.e., tori not contained in strictly bigger tori in G) are conjugate to
each other.
(2) Every g ∈G lies in a maximal torus in G.

Remark 11.2. Here are some remarks on the theorem.

(1) Connectedness of G is crucial.
(2) For connectedness and dimension reasons, every torus in G is obviously contained
in a maximal one.
(3) We showed the second part of the Conjugacy Theorem for the special case G =
SU(n) in HW4.

Example 11.3. The torus

T :=

in G = SU(n) is its own centralizer, as follows from a direct computation by considering
preservation of T-weight spaces in Cn (i.e., any g ∈G that centralizes T must act on Cn in
a way that preserves the isotypic decomposition under T, and those isotypic subspaces are
the standard basis lines which have pairwise distinct T-weights). Thus, T is maximal as a
torus in U(n) (since by commutativity of tori, T is centralized by any torus containing T).

Remark 11.4. The proof of the Conjugacy Theorem will use G-invariant integration on
the manifold G/T. Next time we’ll discuss �

G/H for Lie groups G and closed subgroups

G/H can be non-orientable and in fact can fail to have a G-invariant nonzero regular Borel
measure (we’ll see examples next time).
Fortunately, such difficulties will turn out not to occur for compact connected H (such
as H a compact torus in the context of intended application for the proof of the Conjugacy
Theorem). This will be ultimately because the only continuous homomorphism into R×

from a connected compact group is the trivial homomorphism.

For the rest of today we take G to be a connected compact Lie group, accept the Conjugacy
Theorem as true, and deduce some consequences. We saw above in some examples that a
useful way to prove that a given torus in G is maximal is to show that the torus is its own


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
51

centralizer. The first part of the first corollary below shows that this technique is not only
sufficient but in some sense also necessary.

Corollary 11.5.
(1) A torus T ⊂G is maximal if and only if T = ZG(T).
(2) ZG = �
max’l T T.

Warning 11.6.

�
Note that ZG may be disconnected in Corollary 11.5(2). (For example, if
G = SU(n) then ZG is the diagonally embedded finite group µn of nth roots of unity.)

Proof of Corollary 11.5. For proving (2), choose z ∈ZG and pick a maximal torus T0 ∋z0.
Any maximal T has the form gT0g−1 for some g ∈G by the Conjugacy Theorem, so
T ∋gzg−1 = z.
For the reverse inclusion, pick any g ∈�
max’l T T. For any g0 ∈G, we have g0 ∈T0 for
T0 some maximal torus (by the Conjugacy Theorem). But g ∈T0 by the hypothesis on g
and T0 is commutative, so g and g0 commute. Since g0 ∈G was arbitrary, this implies
g ∈ZG. This completes the proof of (2) in general.
For the proof of (1), one implication is obvious: if T = ZG(T) then T is a maximal
torus because tori are commutative. To prove the converse, we assume T is maximal, pick
g ∈ZG(T), and aim to show that g ∈T. Since T is commutative, T ⊂ZG(g). But T is also
connected, so T ⊂ZG(g)0. Observe that ZG(g)0 is a connected compact Lie group! Further,
T is a maximal torus in ZG(g)0 because T is maximal in G. If we knew g ∈ZG(g)0, then by
centrality of g in ZG(g)0, we could apply Corollary 11.5(2) to ZG(g)0 (!) to conclude g ∈T
as desired.
So, it suffices to show g ∈ZG(g)0. By the Conjugacy Theorem we know g lies in some
maximal torus T′. But T′ is commutative and connected, so g ∈T′ ⊂ZG(g)0.
□

Remark 11.7. In the proof of Corollary 11.5(1), the really key point was to show g ∈ZG(g)0,
and more specifically to find some connected closed subgroup containing g in its center (as
such a subgroup lies in ZG(g)0). One might hope that the connected commutative closed

subgroup ⟨g⟩
0 (where ⟨g⟩:= gZ) does the job, but it can happen that g /∈⟨g⟩

Remark 11.9. For g /∈ZG we know that g ∈ZG(g)0 yet ZG(g)0 ̸= G (since g is assumed
to be non-central in G), so dim ZG(g)0 < dim G. This is sometimes useful for dimension
induction arguments with connected compact Lie groups (to pass to a situation where an
element becomes central).

The next corollary has a level of importance that is impossible to over-estimate, and by
the end of this course you will appreciate how awesomely powerful it is.

Corollary 11.10. For any torus S ⊂G, the group ZG(S) is connected. In particular, if S ̸= 1
then ZG(S)/S is a connected compact Lie group with dim ZG(S)/S < dim G.

Proof. Pick g ∈ZG(S). We know g ∈ZG(g)0, and clearly S ⊂ZG(g), so S ⊂ZG(g)0

because S is connected. Pick a maximal torus S′ ⊂ZG(g)0 containing S. By Corollary 11.5
applied to ZG(g)0 and its central element g, we know that g ∈S′! But S′ ⊂ZG(S)
since S′ is commutative and S′ ⊃S, so S′ ⊂ZG(S)0 because S′ is connected. Thus,
g ∈S′ ⊂ZG(S)0.
□

Remark 11.11. The real significance of Corollary 11.10 arises for non-central tori S ⊂G,
because then ZG(S) is a lower-dimensional connected compact Lie group. The Conjugacy
Theorem provides lots of such S when G is non-commutative (which is equivalent to G
not being a torus because G is compact and connected), since G contains tori through all
elements. Many future dimension induction arguments will involve such passage to torus
centralizers (without losing contact with the connectedness condition!).

Consider a surjective homomorphism of connected compact Lie groups f : G →G.
For a torus S ⊂G and S := f (S) ⊂G, note that S is a connected commutative compact
subgroup of G, so a Lie subgroup by closedness and thus a torus (as the only connected
commutative compact Lie groups are tori). The following really fanstastic result has no
analogue whatsoever in the setting of finite groups; it is a remarkable (and remarkably
useful) result that hinges in an essential way on the connectedness of the groups under
consideration.

Corollary 11.12. In the above setup, the natural map

π : ZG(S) →ZG(S)

is surjective.


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
53

Proof. We know that S is a torus, so by Corollary 11.10 the compact Lie groups ZG(S) and
ZG(S) are connected. Therefore, it is enough to show Lie π is surjective as a map between
the corresponding Lie algebras. Indeed, if a map between Lie groups is surjective on
Lie algebras then it is a submersion near the identity, hence open near the identity and
so globally open (via translation arguments). The image is therefore open, yet an open
subgroup of a topological group is always closed and so must be the entire group in the
connected case. Thus a map of Lie groups having connected target is surjective when it is
surjective between Lie algebras. It is therefore sufficient to show Lie π is surjective since
ZG(S) is connected.
By Corollary H.4.6, for any Lie group H and closed subgroup H′ ⊂H, we have

Lie
�
ZH(H′)
�= hH′

inside h. Thus, Lie(π) is identified with the natural map

gS →gS

induced by the map g →g. By Exercise 5(iii) in HW3 we know that a surjective homomor-
phism between Lie groups is always surjective between Lie algebras (the proof used Sard’s
Theorem), so g →g is surjective. Viewing g as an S-representation (over R) via S →S, it
therefore suffices to show that for any surjection V →V of (finite-dimensional continuous)
S-representations over R, the induced map

VS →V
S

is surjective.
Applying C ⊗R (·) and using that (VΓ)C = (VC)Γ for any group Γ acting linearly on any
R-vector space, it suffices to show the natural map

(VC)S →(V′
C)S

is surjective. But this follows by considering the weight-space decompositions for VC
and V′
C as C-linear representations of S (continuous and finite-dimensional, as always);
i.e., this follows from complete reducibility for torus representations over C (much as
the same holds for isotypic components for any fixed irreducible representation for a
finite group; the subspace of invariant vectors is the isotypic component for the trivial
representation).
□

There is actually no need to extend scalars to C at the end of the preceding proof since
complete reducibility holds just as well over R. However, the device of passing to the
complexified Lie algebras will be an essential tool in many later considerations, so it is
instructive to see it emerge here too (at the very least it simplifies the nature of the torus
representations, even though that isn’t strictly necessary in the present circumstances).

12. INTEGRATION ON COSET SPACES

12.1. Weyl Groups. We’ll start by filling out a loose end on the Conjugacy Theorem.
Consider a connected compact Lie group G and a torus S ⊂G.

W(G, S) := NG(S)/ZG(S) �→Aut(S)

Remark 12.2. Once we have proven the Conjugacy Theorem, for maximal T we’ll have

Example 12.3. Choose n ≥2. Consider G = U(n) and T the diagonal maximal torus


Thinking about the T-weight spaces in Cn, we see that

NG(T) = Sn ⋊T

where Sn is the symmetric group of n × n permutation matrices. The reason is as follows.
On the one hand, clearly Sn ⊂NG(T) (it permutes the standard basis vectors). But any
element of the normalizer permutes the T-weight spaces (being the isotypic subspaces for
the T-action on Cn) which are the standard basis lines and the Sn-subgroup achieves each
such permutation exactly once. So on the other hand, for any n ∈NG(T) there is a unique
σ ∈Sn such that the product σ−1n ∈NG(T) has permutation effect that is trivial (and
hence lies in the diagonal subgroup of U(n) that in turn is exactly T; check!). Thus,

W(G, T) = Sn.

Remark 12.4. In Appendix K, the following are proved:

(1) For n ≥2 and G′ := SU(n) with maximal diagonal torus T′, once again W(G′, T′) =
Sn but it is not possible to lift this Weyl group isomorphically into NG′(T′). For
more details, see §K.2.
(2) Granting the Conjugacy Theorem, so ZG(S) is connected, W(G, S) is finite if and
only if NG(S)0 = ZG(S). In Proposition K.4.1, the finiteness of W(G, S) is directly
proved (not conditional on anything). This will be used for maximal S in the proof
of the Conjugacy Theorem.

Remark 12.5. Why intuitively should the Weyl group be finite? The idea of the finitness
of W(G, S) = NG(S)/ZG(S) ⊂Aut(S) is as follows. There is an anti-equivalence of
categories

{tori} ↔{finite free Z-modules}

S �→X(S) = Hom(S, S1).

Since Hom(S, S1) ≃Zr for S =
�
S1�r, Aut(S) ≃Aut (X(S))opp ≃GLr(Z) is naturally
“discrete”. But its subgroup NG(S)/ZG(S) is naturally compact, and a compact discrete
space is finite!
Of course, this is not actually a proof. The issue is that we have not established a rigorous
link between the compact topology of NG(S) and the ad-hoc discrete topology on GLr(Z).
The details for such a justification are given in the proof of finiteness of W(G, S).

12.2. Invariant integration on G/H. We’ll now develop the notion of invariant integra-

Let G be a Lie group (we are not assuming G is compact) and let H ⊂G be a closed
subgroup. Then, G/H is a C∞manifold (recall that G/H has a unique C∞-structure
making G →G/H a submersion yielding the expected tangent space at the identity coset).
Let µG and µH be left Haar measures on G and H respectively.

Question 12.7. Does there exist a nonzero regular Borel measure µ on G/H that is G-
invariant (using the natural left-translation action of G on G/H)?

In favorable situations such a Borel measure µ exists with uniqueness properties similar

Fact 12.8.
(1) By [Lang, Ch. XII, Theorem 4.3], if a G-invariant µ exists then after unique
R>0-scaling the Fubini relation (12.1) holds for all f ∈Cc(G). By [Lang, Ch. XII,
Theorem 4.1], the map

f �→f

is surjective, so by the Riesz representation theorem the Fubini relation uniquely
determines µ given the choices of µG and µH (as µG controls the left side of (12.1)
and µH controls the definition of f �→f). In particular, this gives a unique charac-
terization of µ without direct reference to the G-invariance property.
(2) By [Nach, p. 138], µ exists if and only if ∆G|H = ∆H.

13. PROOF OF CONJUGACY THEOREM I

Let G be a connected compact Lie group, T ⊂G a maximal torus. Consider the map
(easily seen to be C∞; check!):

q: (G/T) × T →G
(13.1)

(g, t) �→gtg−1.

This is a C∞map between connected compact smooth manifolds that are orientable (since
we saw last time that for any Lie group G and closed subgroup H, the smooth manifold
G/H admits a nowhere-vanishing even G-invariant top-degree smooth differential form
when H is connected and compact) and clearly have the same dimension. Next time, we’ll
combine coset integration �

G/T with techniques developed today to show q is surjective.
This is sufficient for our needs:

Proposition 13.1. If q is surjective then the Conjugacy Theorem holds for G.

Proof. The Conjugacy Theorem (Theorem 11.1) has two parts. Let’s show that each part
follows from q being surjective.

(2) For any g0 ∈G there exist g ∈G, t ∈T so that g0 = gtg−1 ∈gTg−1. Since gTg−1 is
a (maximal) torus, this proves any element of G lies in a (maximal) torus.
(1) Pick a maximal torus T′. We seek some g ∈G so that gT′g−1 = T (which is
equivalent to T′ = g−1Tg, and is the same as showing T′ ⊂g−1Tg since T′ is
maximal; this in turn is equivalent to showing gT′g−1 ⊂T). To achieve this, we
seek a sufficiently generic element t′ ∈T′ in the sense that that ⟨t′⟩= T′. If we
have such a t′ then surjectivity of q provides g ∈G so that gt′g−1 ∈T. Hence,
g⟨t′⟩g−1 ⊂T so passing to closures gives gT′g−1 ⊂T, which is enough for our
purposes. To find such a t′, we use Lemma 13.2 below.
□

Lemma 13.2 (Density Lemma). For any torus S, there exists s ∈S so that ⟨s⟩= S.

Example 13.3. Take S = S1. Then any element not of finite order works.

Proof of Density Lemma. The idea is that for any s ∈S, ⟨s⟩contains ⟨s⟩
0 as a torus of finite
index in ⟨s⟩. Furthermore, we claim there are only countably many proper subtori in S.
Indeed, because X(S) ≃Zr has only countably many torsion-free quotients (corresponding
to saturated Z-submodules), the countability claim follows from the categorical anti-
equivalence between tori and finite free Z-modules. Therefore, there are only countably
many possibilities for ⟨s⟩if it is a proper subgroup of S, and those are all closed subgroups
of S, hence nowhere-dense. The Baire Category Theorem thereby gives the desired s. See
§K.5 for full details.
□

By Proposition 13.1, to prove the Conjugacy Theorem it suffices to show the map q in
(13.1) is surjective.

Question 13.4. How to show q : (G/T) × T →G is surjective?

Our method will be to consider more generally a C∞map f : M′ →M between compact
connected oriented manifolds of the same dimension d > 0. We’ll define an invariant
deg( f ) ∈Z so that:

(2) this “degree” is computable in some useful sense.

exists a unique number deg( f ) ∈Z so that for all ω ∈Ωtop(M),

Lemma 13.6. For f : M′ →M a C∞map of compact connected oriented smooth manifolds, if
deg f ̸= 0 then f is surjective.

14. PROOF OF THE CONJUGACY THEOREM II

Today, we’ll wrap up the proof of the Conjugacy Theorem. Let’s begin by reviewing
notation and results from last time. Let G be a connected compact Lie group, T ⊂G a
maximal torus, W the group NG(T)/T. We do not yet know T = ZG(T), so we don’t
know W = W(G, T), but we see in Appendix K that W is finite. In particular, we know
NG(T)0 = T; this will be very important near the end today.
Last time we defined the C∞map

q: (G/T) × T →G
(g, t) �→g.t

with g.t just notation for gtg−1 for any representative g of g. We reduced the proof of the
Conjugacy Theorem to showing q is surjective. Further, we saw last time in Lemma 13.6
that it is enough to show deg(q) ̸= 0 for a choice of orientations on G, T, G/T. Now choose
t0 ∈T such that ⟨t0⟩= T; we’ll check that we can apply the formula “deg = ∑εj” from
Lemma 13.8 using the fiber q−1(t0) over t0.


62
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Remark 14.1. Later it will turn out that the condition “⟨t0⟩= T” on t0 for applicability
of Lemma 13.8 to its fiber (to be justified below for t0 as above) can be considerably
weakened to just a “Zariski-open” condition on t0 (corresponding to “distinct eigenvalues”
for G = U(n)), but we don’t need such refinement at this time.

Lemma 14.2. We have

q−1(t0) =
��
w, w−1.t0
�
: w ∈W
�
.

In particular, q−1(t0) is finite of size #W.

Proof. Consider some g ∈G and t ∈T with gtg−1 = t0. Then,

T = ⟨t0⟩= g⟨t⟩g−1 ⊂gTg−1.

The torus gTg−1 is a torus of the same dimension as T that contains T. This implies
T = gTg−1, so g ∈NG(T) and hence g ∈W. Likewise, clearly w.t = t0, so t = w−1.t0.
□

In order to prove the Conjugacy Theorem by the degree method from last time, it remains
to show that for each

ξ := (g, t) ∈q−1(t0)

the tangent map

dq(ξ): Tg (G/T) × Tt(T) = Tξ ((G/T) × T) →Tt0(G)

is an isomorphism and even orientation-preserving. That is, for oriented bases (to make
sense of a “determinant”) we just need to show det (dq(ξ)) > 0. To proceed, we next have
to specify orientations, by defining suitable nowhere-vanishing top-degree differental
forms on G, T, and G/T.
We will see below that G/T is even-dimensional, so in the end it won’t for matter if
we switch the order of G/T and T when defining a product orientation on (G/T) × T
(switching the factors in a product of oriented connected smooth manifolds with respective
dimensions d and d′ changes the product orientation by (−1)dd′). But we will be precise
about our orientation definitions during the argument anyway (since we don’t yet know
dim(G/T) is even).

14.1. Finishing the proof of the Conjugacy Theorem. Fix top-degree differential forms
dg on G and dt on T that are nonzero and left-invariant (hence they are bi-invariant, since
the groups are compact and connected). We emphasize that these are differential forms,
not measures.
We have seen earlier that a (preferred) left-invariant nonzero top-degree differential
form dg on G/T is specified by the condition that the canonical isomorphism

Ωtop
G/T(e) ⊗Ωtop
T (e) →Ωtop
G (e)

satisfies (dg)(e) ⊗(dt)(e) �→(dg)(e). Since dg is nonzero at e and left-invariant, it is
nowhere-vanishing and so defines an orientation on G/T.
We now get top-degree C∞differential forms q∗(dg), dg ∧dt on (G/T) × T, the second
of which is nowhere-vanishing, so

q∗(dg) = J (dg ∧dt)

for some J ∈C∞((G/T) × T) .


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
63

Unravelling definitions, our task is exactly to show J(ξ) > 0 for all ξ ∈q−1(t0). This
positivity is immediate from Proposition 14.4 stated below, whose formulation requires us
to first introduce some notation:

Notation 14.3. For a Lie group G and a closed subgroup H ⊂G, define the map

AdG/H : H →GL (Te (G/H))

h �→dℓh(e)

that is clearly a C∞homomorphism.
It is important to note that both maps ℓh, ch : G/H →G/H which preserve the point e
since right-translation rh−1 on G induces the identity map on G/H. Hence, dℓh(e) = dch(e)
on g/h is induced by dch(e) = AdG(e) on g for ch : G →G that fixes e (whereas ℓh on G
generally does not fix e). Therefore, the map AdG/H on g/h is really induced by AdG|H
on g, explaining the notation.

Now it remains to show:

15. WEYL INTEGRATION FORMULA AND ROOTS

Last time we finished the proof of the Conjugacy Theorem. Let’s record one more
consequence of that and then move on. As is our typical setup, let G be a connected
compact Lie group and T ⊂G a maximal torus. We know that T = ZG(T), and the
resulting group

W := W(G, T) = NG(T)/ZG(T) = NG(T)/T

is finite. Appendix M gives details of the following application.


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
65

Let Conj(G) := G/ ∼with x ∼y if there is g ∈G with gxg−1 ∼y; this is the set of
conjugacy classes in G, and we equip it with the quotient topology from G. The continuous
composite map T →G →Conj(G) is invariant under the W-action on T (as that is induced
by NG(T)-conjugation on G), so we obtain a continuous map of quotient spaces

T/W
α−→Conj(G).

In fact, this map α is a homeomorphism of compact Hausdorff spaces, so it induces an
isomorphism of C-algebras

The proof of injectivity of the map α uses the Conjugacy Theorem for ZG(t)0 for any t ∈T.
(See Appendix M for more details on the preceding assertions.)

occuring in any representation of G over C).

16. ROOT SYSTEMS AND CLIFFORD ALGEBRAS

Let’s start by recalling what we obtained last time. As usual, consder a connected
compact Lie group G and a maximal torus T. Let W = W(G, T) = NG(T)/T. We made
a W-stable finite subset Φ ⊂X(T) −{0} giving the nontrivial T-weights on gC with its
AdG-action of T. Also, Φ ⊂X(T/ZG) because the center ZG of G acts trivially under the


70
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

adjoint representation (since AdG is given by differentiating the conjugation action of G
on itself, under which ZG acts trivially). Note that the torus T is its own centralizer on G
(by maximality), so it contains ZG (and hence T/ZG makes sense).
Keep in mind that although elements of

X(T) := Hom(T, S1)

are of “multiplicative” nature, we will often use the notation tχ := χ(t) and thereby
write the “multiplication” in X(T) additively. One reason for the additive notation is that
historically people worked much more on the Lie algebra level than the Lie group level,
and that is akin to working with logarithms (i.e., going backwards through the exponential
map). But there are other reasons that additive notation will be convenient, as we shall see.
Let’s review the outcome of calculations from last time:

Example 16.1. For G = U(n) and T the diagonal torus (S1)n we saw Φ = −Φ. We also
saw that dim(gC)a = 1 for all a ∈Φ.
Explicitly, for

ZG =: Z = S1 ∆−→(S1)n = T ⊂U(n) = G,

we have
�

ai −aj : i ̸= j
�

for the projections ai : T = (S1)n →S1, so tai−aj = ti/tj for all t ∈T = (S1)n. Observe also
that ZΦ = X(T/Z).
The natural action of W = Sn on X(T) = Zn is the usual coordinate permutation (this is
immediate from the definition of how W acts on X(T) and the identification of W with the
group of n × n permutation matrices in U(n) (whose conjugation effect on a diagonal torus
is to permute the diagonal entries in accordance with the corresponding element of Sn).
The general fact that the W-action on X(T) preserves Φ (which holds just by unwinding
definitions) is thereby seen quite vividly in the case of U(n).
Another nice feature of the case of U(n) is that for all a ∈Φ,

(Qa) ∩Φ = {±a}

inside X(T)Q, as we see by inspection. Later we’ll see that this holds in complete generality;
it is not at all obvious (e.g., in general why couldn’t there be a, b ∈Φ so that 2a = 3b, or in
other words two distinct non-trivial T-weights χ, χ′ on gC such that χ2 = χ′3?). Further,
we computed the weight space explicitly for each element of Φ(U(n), T), getting

(gC)ai−aj = CEij

where Eij is the matrix with a 1 in the ij-entry and 0’s elsewhere via the identification

u(n)C ≃gln(C) = Matn(C).

We’ll next work out the analogue of Example 16.1 for SU(n), not from scratch but rather
by explaining conceptually why much of the output of the U(n)-calculation must agree
with analogues for SU(n) (the latter case being a bit less explicit since the Weyl group for
SU(n) is not liftable to a subgroup of the normalizer of the maximal torus, in contrast with
the case of U(n) via permutation matrices). The reason for working out the relationship

the actions of T′ �→T, so by chasing weights under T = Z ˙T′ on the complexifications we
obtain Φ′ = Φ inside X (T′/Z′) = X (T/Z).
Rationalizing, we obtain a canonical isomorphism

X(Z)Q ⊕X(T′)Q
(†)
←X(T)Q.

(In general passing to rationalizations of character lattices gets rid of discrepancies caused
by isogenies between tori.) Since T′ = T ∩G′ with G′ normal in G, and T = T′ · Z with
Z = ZG central in G, we obtain

W′ := NG′(T′)/T′ = NG(T)/T =: W.

Explicitly, this equality W′ = W is compatible with their actions on the two sides of (†),
with the W-action on X(Z)Q being trivial (since Z is central in G). Compatibly with (†)
we also have {0} × Φ′ = Φ.

Remark 16.5. In the general case, the role of SU(n) will be replaced by the commutator
subgroup G′ := [G, G] which turns out (not at all obviously!) to be closed, and G′ will turn
out to satisfy G′ = [G′, G′] (so it is “very non-commutative”!). Likewise, Z above will be
replaced with either ZG or Z0
G (the maximal central torus), depending on the situation.

Since T/Z0
G →T/ZG is an isogeny of tori, we have

X(T/ZG)Q ≃X(T/Z0
G)Q.

We’ll later show in general for any (G, T), the following properties hold:

• the pair (X(T/ZG)Q, Φ) is a reduced root system (a concept to be defined later, but
purely combinatorial and admitting a development on its own without reference to
connected compact Lie groups),
• the subgroup W �→GL(X(T/ZG)Q) = GL(X(T′)Q) (injective because W = W′

and ZG′(T′) = T′!) is generated by certain special “reflections” of the Q-vector
space X(T/ZG)Q = X(T/Z0
G)Q = X(T′)Q.

Warning 16.6.

�

This is very bad! One should (as in [Bou2]) keep the vector space rational until the real

Remark 16.7. The viewpoint of root systems (X(T/ZG)Q, Φ) will be very convienient for
analyzing the reprsentation theory of G later.

We’ve talked so far about SU(n), SO(2m + 1), Sp(n), SO(2m) (which are called type
A,B,C,D respectively, due to a classification theorem for irreducible root systems to be
discussed later). In HW5 Exercise 4 we saw that SU(n) and Sp(n) are simply connected,
whereas for n ≥3 we saw that #π1(SO(n)) ≤2. For SO(n) it turns out that there is
a connected degree-2 covering space, known as the Spin group Spin(n) (so π1(SO(n)) =
Z/2Z), as we now discuss.
Here is some preliminary motivation (so don’t worry too much about omitted details).
For connected Lie groups G and discrete normal subgroups Γ ⊂G (necessarily central by

for some connected Lie group Spin(n) (necessarily compact since it is a finite-degree cover-

double cover is built.

Example 16.8. If n = 3, we have an explicit connected double cover

SU(2) →SO(3)

(so π1(SO(3)) = Z/2Z).

Remark 16.9. In class, Brian performed the “belt trick.” Essentially, he took off his belt,
had a tall(er) volunteer hold one end of the belt rigidly in space in their outstretched
hand, he held the other end to make the belt essentially horizontal to the ground, and
he walked in a loop around the other person while lifting the belt over their head and
bringing his end of the belt back to its beginning position and orientation. This left the
belt with a twist, and he then walked in another loop in the same direction except now
going under the volunteer’s outstretched arm (walking in front of them), after which the
twisting disappeared!
How exactly does this illustrate that π1(SO(3)) contains a non-trivial 2-torsion element?
Visualize an element of SO(3) as an orthonormal frame, and think about one such frame
assigned uniformly at all points along a line segment on the entire top of the belt. Thinking


74
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

of a path in SO(3) as a continuous family of orthonormal frames, the end of the belt in
Brian’s hand is sweeping out the same closed loop σ in SO(3) both times he walks around
in a circle (a closed loop since Brian’s end of the belt returns to its original position in space
at the end of both circular trips). But at any fixed point ξ of the belt away from the ends
the first walk-around is not a closed loop in SO(3) due to the twist (which prevents the
orthonormal frame based at ξ from returning to its original position in space). However,
after going around twice, the motion of each point ξ of the belt is a closed loop in SO(3) (the
corresponding orthonormal frame at ξ returning to its initial position) precisely because
the belt ends up untwisted after the double walk-around.
Tracing out what is happening to the orthonormal frame at each point ξ of the belt
during the entire process thereby gives a “continuous family” of closed loops linking the
closed loop σ ∗σ in SO(3) at Brian’s endpoint of the belt to the identity loop in SO(3) at the
other end of the belt (the rigid hand of the volunteer). In other words, this entire process
visualizes the homotopy in SO(3) between σ ∗σ and the identity. (The fact that σ ̸∼1 in
π1(SO(3)) is not rigorously justified by this visualization.)
There are a lot of videos of the “belt trick” on the Internet, generally done in a very
different way which completely misses the point of the mathematical content (i.e., visualiz-
ing the homotopy to the identity of the square of the generator of π1(SO(3))). Some day
Tadashi Tokieda will do a proper job of this in an Internet video, perhaps on Numberphile,
and in the meantime here are his illustrations of the 3 main steps of the process in reverse
(corresponding to doing the walk-around under the arm of the volunteer first, and then
going around the volunteer with the belt passing over their head, with the stool serving in
the role of the rigid hand of the volunteer):

FIGURE 3. T. Tokieda’s illustration of the belt trick from class in reverse

details on this are discussed in Appendix P.

17. TORUS CENTRALIZERS AND SU(2)

Today we will find out why SU(2) is the secret to life! We’ll discuss central tori in
connected compact Lie groups and explain why SU(2) is extremely ubiquitous beyond the
commutative case.
For today, let G be a connected compact Lie group, T ⊂G a maximal torus, Z := Z0
G
the maximal central torus (since a torus is the same as a connected compact commutative
Lie group). Defining G′ := [G, G] to be the commutator subgroup (in the purely algebraic
sense), later we’ll show G′ is a closed subgroup with (G′)′ = G′ (i.e., G′ is “perfect”) and

that the natural homomorphism Z × G′ mult

is an isogeny).

Example 17.1. In HW7, we’ll see that every g ∈SU(2) is a commutator, and this will turn
out to imply SU(n)′ = SU(n). Thus, the isogeny ZU(n) × SU(n) →U(n) considered last
time is a special case of the general isogeny mentioned above.

One might ask when we would encounter G with nontrivial Z. So far, many of the

Z0

Let G be non-commutative. If we take any noncentral torus S ⊂G, the torus centralizer
ZG(S) is connected (as a consequence of the Conjugacy Theorem). Note that S ⊂G is
contained in a maximal torus T′, so ZG(S) ⊃T′. However, when S is chosen in a way
related to Φ(G, T) that we will discuss soon, ZG(S) will often be non-commutative (and
it has S as a central torus by design). Such centralizers will be a ubiquitous tool in the
structural study of connected compact Lie groups.

Let’s begin the proof of Theorem 17.8; we’ll finish it next time. Note that dim (G/T) > 0
and is even, so dim G/T ≥2, so dim G ≥3.

Lemma 17.11. Theorem 17.8 holds in the case dim G = 3.

Proof. Fix a G-invariant positive-definite quadratic form q on g (acted upon by the compact
Lie group G via AdG). Thus, by connectedness of G we have

G

where SO(3) = SO(q) = O(q)0 is 3-dimensional. But

(ker AdG)0 = Z0

is a proper closed subgroup since T is not central (as G is non-commutative), so Z0

Thus, ker AdG is finite.
But dim G = 3 = dim SO(q), so for dimension reasons the inclusion G/(ker AdG) ⊂
SO(q) = SO(3) is an equality. Hence, G →SO(3) is an isogeny and more specifically a
connected finite-degree covering space. Now form a pullback diagram of covering maps
as compact (perhaps disconnected!) Lie groups:

(17.3)

H
SU(2) = S3

G
SO(3)

with the vertical maps of degree 2. Suppose the bottom horizontal map is not an isomor-
phism (i.e., it is a finite-degree covering space with degree > 1). Then the top horizontal
map is also a finite-degree covering map with degree > 1. But SU(2) = S3 is simply
connected, so its covering space H must be disconnected.
Hence, for the degree-2 covering map H →G, it follows that the open and closed
subset H0 ⊂H must be a lower-degree covering space over G and so has to be degree
1! This gives H0 ≃G. But H0 →SU(2) is a connected finite-degree covering space of
SU(2) = S3, so it must also have degree 1 and hence is an isomorphism. We conclude that
if AdG : G →SO(3) is not an isomorphism then G ≃H0 ≃SU(2).
□

18. COMMUTATORS AND RANK-1 SUBGROUPS

Last time we began the proof of the following crucial result.

Theorem 18.1. Let G be a connected compact Lie group with rank 1 that is non-commutative.
Then G ≃SU(2) or G ≃SO(3).


80
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Recall that the rank of a compact connected Lie group is the dimension of its maximal
tori.

Proof. Note that dim (G/T) > 0 and is even, so dim G/T ≥2, so dim G ≥3. Last time, in
Lemma 17.11, we settled the case dim G = 3. So, we just have to show dim G = 3.
To this end, choose T ⊂G a maximal torus (with dim T = 1 by assumption). Consider
the weight space decomposition

gC = tC ⊕(⊕α∈Φ (gC)a) .

with Φ := Φ(G, T) ̸= ∅. We want to show dimC (gC) = 3. It is enough to show that
Φ = {±a0} for some a0 ∈X(T) −{0} with dimC (gC)a = 1 for a ∈{±a0}.
We’ll now highlight the key points. For full details, see Appendix Q. We’ll connect our
task to the representation theory of sl2(C) that has been discussed earlier.
To start, we give a direct argument for why the roots show up in opposite pairs (though
we’re not yet claiming that opposite roots have weight spaces of the same dimension, let
alone of dimension 1).

Lemma 18.2. We have Φ = −Φ.

Proof. Note that T = S1 acts on g/t. This is a (continuous) representation over R, and as
such is completely reducible (as for any compact group, due to averaging against Haar
measure). When this representation is tensored up to C it has only nontrivial weights (since
gT
C = tC and the T-action on gC is completely reducible). Hence, we want to understand
the irreducible nontrivial representations of S1 over R.
The irreducible nontrivial representations ρ of S1 over R are precisely the standard
2-dimensional representation (acting by rotations on R2) precomposed with some power
map z �→zn for n ∈Z>0. To see this, one uses knowledge of the complex representation
theory and then examines which representations have a character preserved under complex
conjugation (and then one has to build a suitable descent over R, namely from the asserted
list of possibilities). This is carried out in HW7, Exercise 5(ii).
By inspection, ρC = χ ⊕χ−1 for some χ ∈X(S1); note that this consists of a character
and its reciprocal. Thus, the same holds for (ρ ◦zn)C for any n ∈Z. Therefore, by complete
reducibility of S1-representations over R, we are done.
□

Now, pick the highest weight a0 ∈Φ ⊂X(T) = X(S1) = Z. (Here we are fixing an
isomorphism T ≃S1.) Since T acts on each weight space through a C×-valued character,
for any a, b ∈X(T) we have

[(gC)a, (gC)b] ⊂(gC)a+b

(interesting only for a, b ∈Φ, but note that it can happen that a + b = 0, in which case
the right side is tC). Using that [·, ·]g ̸= 0 and that G has rank 1, one can show (using
1-parameter subgroups) that the subspace
�
(gC)a0 , (gC)−a0

�
⊂tC

is nonzero and so exhausts tC.
Then via suitable C×-scaling, we can find X± ∈(gC)±a0 and H ∈tC so that {X+, X−, H}
is an sl2-triple (i.e., their span is sl2(C) with the usual bracket relations).


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
81

Remark 18.3. We are doing linear algebra at the complexified level gC! The elements X±
and H we have made have no reason whatsoever to lie in g, so this has no direct meaning
in terms of G. In particular, we’re not claiming H ∈t (only in tC). Working with such
elements is like an out-of-body experience.

Now we have a Lie subalgebra sl2(C) �→gC whose diagonal is the line tC. This
has the special feature that the 0-weight space for tC on gC (in the sense of Lie-algebra
representations) is just tC since connectedness of G and T forces zg(t) = Lie(ZG(T)) = t,
and tC is a line. This is a very small weight-0 space. Moreover, we can view gC as an
sl2(C)-representation, and by design gC has a0 as its highest weight. Therefore, by design
of the sl2(C)-subalgebra, all weights for H on gC lie in {−2, −1, 0, 1, 2}.
Since the representation theory of sl2(C) is completely reducible, and the copy of sl2(C)
inside gC already accounts for the entire weight-0 space for H, there is no room for the
occurrence of the trivial representation inside gC. We now look at the list of nontrivial
irreducible representations of sl2(C). For weight reasons, the only options that can occur
inside gC are sl2(C) and the standard 2-dimensional representation std2. The former can
only occur once since the weight-0 space for H in gC is a line, so

gC = sl2(C) ⊕(std2)⊕N

as sl2(C)-representations for some N ≥0. In §Q.1 it is shown (again using that G has rank
1) that N = 0. This completes the proof.
□

multiplication), so G′ = G;
(2) if G = SO(3) and G′ = SU(2) then (S × G′)/µ ≃G, where µ := S ∩G′ = {±1} ⊂
SU(2) = G′ is anti-diagonally embedded in S × G′.

Remark 18.5. To give some intuition for this result, because SU(2) and so also its quotient
SO(3) coincides with its own commutator subgroup we may hope that the commutator
G′ = [G, G] should give a “section up to isogeny” G →G. A general principle is to use
commutators to build an isogeny complement to a central subgroup (a principle that
will acquire more substance when we build on the current work to tackle commutator
subgroups in higher-rank situations later).


82
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Sketch of proof. The full details are in §2 of the handount on SU(2). The idea of the proof is
to build an “isogeny-section” to the exact sequence by using Lie algebras. Let’s first focus
on the Lie algebras. We have

(18.3)
0
s
g
g ≃su(2)
0

in which s is central in g (in the Lie algebra sense!) because S is central in G. We claim that
this exact sequence uniquely splits.
Note that su(2) is its own commutator subalgebra (i.e., brackets generate all of su(2))
since it suffices to check this at the complexified level and su(2)C = sl2(C) (and one can
check explicitly that usual basis vectors for weight spaces in sl2(C) are brackets among
Q×-multiplies of the standard basis vectors for weight spaces). Therefore, checking that
the above sequence of Lie algebras splits is equivalent to checking that the natural map
[g, g] →g is an isomorphism. This also shows that a splitting is unique if it exists.
This isomorphism criterion for splitting is equivalent to the same after complexifying,
and [g, g]C = [gC, gC]. Complexifying the exact sequence gives an exact sequence

(18.4)
0
sC
gC
gC
0

in which sC →gC is central and gC = sl2(C). This makes gC a representation of sl2(C)
(using that sC is central: the Lie bracket always factors through the quotient by any central
Lie subalgebra), so it makes gC into a Lie-algebra representation of sl2(C).
Now by complete reducibility of the representation theory of sl2(C), we get the unique
splitting (since there is only room in gC for one copy of sl2(C))! Hence, (18.3) splits as
a sequence over R. This gives a section su(2) →g. Since the Lie group SU(2) is simply
connected, this section integrates to a Lie group map h : SU(2) →G that in turn is an
inclusion at the Lie algebra level. Therefore, this map has kernel which is a 0-dimensional
normal discrete subgroup, hence central and finite. The center of SU(2) is {±1}. Therefore,
the kernel of h is either 1 or {±1}, so either SU(2) or SU(2)/{±1} = SO(3) is thereby
found as a closed subgroup of G.
We have identified SU(2) or SO(3) with a closed subgroup H ⊂G for which S ∩H
is finite and central in H, making H an isogeny complement to S! In particular, H =
[H, H] = [G, G], the final equality because S is central (the commutator map Γ × Γ →Γ for
any group Γ factors through a map (Γ/Z) × (Γ/Z) →Γ for any central subgroup Z ⊂Γ).
Hence, the commutator subgroup [G, G] is closed! The rest of the argument is now some
bookkeeping, and is given in Appendix Q.
□

Remark 18.6. Did you know that “bookkeeping” is one of the few words in the English
language with three consecutive pairs of double letters? Another one is “bookkeeper”. Yet
another is “bookkeepers”! There are no others, but I hereby patent the word “ibookkeeper”
so that if Apple invents an app to destroy the accounting industry then I can blackmail
them.

As is worked out in HW7, under a central quotient map between connected compact Lie
groups, the formation of image and preimage give bijections between the sets of maximal
tori in each. (This is immediate from the Conjugacy Theorem, and uses that maximal tori
always contain the center and that the image of a maximal torus under a quotient map is a
maximal torus.) Note in particular that the preimage of a maximal torus under a central
quotient map between connected compact Lie groups is always connected!

obvious from the Conjugacy Theorem), it follows that T′

It turns out that W = S3 acts as D3 (the dihedral group of order 6) on that regular
hexagon whose vertices are the roots. (This calculation of the Weyl group, and for all
special unitary groups more generally, will be taken up later.) Although there is no
Euclidean structure instrinsic to our situation with X(T)Q, there is a precise sense in which
ra deserves to be called a reflection:

Definition 19.6. Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space over a field k of characteristic 0
(we will often care about k = Q, R, C). A reflection on V is an isomorphism r : V ≃V so
that r2 = id with −1-eigenspace a line (so the 1-eigenspace is complementary hyperplane).

We emphasize that the −1-eigenline alone doesn’t determine the complemtary hyper-
plane that is the 1-eigenspace of r since there is no “Euclidean structure” (non-degenerate
quadratic form, say) specified that is preserved by r.

BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

Example 19.7. Take V = X(T)Q with ra acting as −1 on the eigenline X(T′
a)Q and as 1 on
the hyperplane X(Ta)Q.

Note that ra ∈W ⊂GL (X(T)Q) with W finite. This allows us to apply the following
result to show that from the specification of W the element ra is determined by the line on
which it acts as negation (“as if” there were a Euclidean structure, which there is not), and
likewise is determined by the hyperplane on which it acts as the identity.

Lemma 19.8. Let Γ ⊂GL(V) be a finite subgroup and r, r′ ∈Γ two reflections. If r and r′ negate
the same line, or alternatively act as 1 on the same hyperplane, then r = r′.

Proof. Suppose they act as −1 on the same line L. Consider the exact sequence

(19.2)
0
L
V
V/L
0

that is compatible with each of r and r′ acting on V, so we can consider the effect of
r′r−1 ∈Γ on this situation. The effect on L in (−1)2 = 1, and its effect on V/L is also
1, so r′r−1 is unipotent, necessarily of finite order (since Γ is finite). By the same type of
argument, if r and r′ act as the identity on the same line then again r′r−1 is unipotent,
necessarily of finite order.
But over a field of characteristic 0, a unipotent matrix of finite order is trivial (exercise,
using log(1 + N) for nilpotent matrices N)! Hence, r′r−1 = id, so r′ = r.
□

Proof. By definition, Ta = (ker a)0, so a|Ta = 1. This means the projection to X(Ta)Q is 0.

Corollary 20.2. We have ra(a) = −a, and this characterizes the element ra ∈W(G, T) as a
reflection on X(T)Q.

The point of this corollary is that it characterizes ra in terms of a rather directly, without
reference to the specific construction involving the pair (G′
a, T′
a).

Proof. This is immediate from Lemma 20.1 and Lemma 19.8 (with Γ = W(G, T)).
□

Here are some more consequences.

Corollary 20.3. We have:

(1) for a, b ∈Φ, ra = rb if and only if b = ±a;
(2) on X(T)Q, ra(x) = x −ℓa(x)a for a unique linear form ℓa ∈X(T)∗
Q (so ℓa(a) = 2 since
ra(a) = −a).


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
89

Proof. We prove these in order.

(1) The implication “⇐” holds because ra and r−a are reflections in the finite group
W(G, T) that negate the same line (so Lemma 19.8 forces ra = r−a); this could also
seen from the construction using (G′
a, T′
a), but it is much more elegant to argue via
Lemma 19.8. For the converse implication, if ra = rb then the lines negated by these
reflections coincide, hence Qa = Qb, so b ∈Φ ∩Qa = {±a}.
(2) Via the decomposition X(T)Q = X(Ta)Q ⊕X(T′
a)Q for which ra acts as the identity
on the hyperplane X(Ta)Q, we see that ra(x) −x ∈X(T′
a)Q = Qa for all x. Express-
ing the coefficient of a as ℓa(x)a for a function ℓa : X(T)Q →Q, clearly ℓa is linear
and is unique.
□

Miracle 20.4. It will turn out that ℓa(Φ) ⊂Z and even better: ℓa(X(T)) ⊂Z. We’ll give
a Lie group interpretation of this via co-characters X∗(T) = Hom(S1, T) later today, in
terms of which this important integrality property will be established.

Remark 20.5. In HW7 you’ll show that the finite subgroup ZG/Z0
G ⊂G/Z0
G is the center
of G/Z0
G (which is perhaps quite surprising, since in general passing to a quotient of a
group can lead to the center of the quotient being bigger than the image of the original
center; think about a non-abelian solvable group). Moreover, in HW7 you’ll show the
following are equivalent:

(1) ZG is finite (which is to say Z0
G = 1, or in other words G has no nontrivial central
torus);
(2) QΦ = X(T)Q (i.e., Φ spans X(T)Q over Q);
(3) the Z-span ZΦ (called the “root lattice”) inside the “character lattice” X(T) has
finite index (then necessarily equal to #ZG).
Further, it will be shown in HW8 that these conditions imply G = G′ (i.e., G is its own
commutator subgroup, so it is “very non-commutative”). The converse is also true: if
G = G′ then the above conditions all hold (this will be shown in HW9, where it will also
be proved that G′ is always closed in G).

TABLE 1. Centers of classical groups

Recall that we always have that the faithful action of W on X(T) preserving Φ ⊂
X(T) −{0}, so W acts faithfully on X(T)Q also preserving the image of Φ in X(T)Q. This
entire discussion finally brings us to an important general concept:

Definition 20.7. Let k be a field of characteristic 0. A root system over k is a pair (V, Φ) for
a finite-dimensional k-vector space V and a finite set Φ ⊂V −{0} (called roots) so that:


90
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

RS1 the k-span kΦ is equal to V (so V = 0 ⇐⇒Φ = ∅)
RS3 for all a ∈Φ, there is a reflection ra : V ≃V satisfying

(a) ra(a) = −a (so −Φ = Φ);
(b) ra(Φ) = Φ;

(c) writing ra(x) = x −ℓa(x)a for a unique ℓa ∈V∗, we have ℓa(Φ) ⊂Z (this is
called the Integrality Axiom).

A root system is called reduced if it additionally satisfies

RS2 ka ∩Φ = {±a}.

We will always work with reduced root systems because the root systems that arise from
connected compact Lie groups will turn out to be reduced. But there are good reasons
to care about non-reduced root systems. For example, when classifying (or analyzing
the structure of) non-compact connected Lie groups with a “semisimple” Lie algebra or
connected “semisimple” linear algebraic groups over general fields (a notion that is most
appropriately defined using ideas from algebraic geometry), there is always an associated
root system (this is a rather hard fact!) but it can be non-reduced (when the group is
“non-split”, a basic example being special unitary groups over R with mixed signature).
It may seem in the definition of a root system that the reflection ra is somehow ambiguous
or is extra data to be specified. Let’s see that such a reflection (if it exists) is uniquely
determined by (V, Φ) and the required properties. The key point is that

ra ∈Γ := {T ∈GL(V) : T(Φ) = Φ}

with Φ a finite spanning set of V, and Γ is a finite group (since a linear automorphism is
determined by its effect on a spanning set, and there are only finitely many permutations
of the finite set Φ). Hence, ra is uniquely determined by the further requirement that it
negates the line ka (here we are again using Lemma 19.8). Further, as we have seen in the
Lie group setting, for any root system (V, Φ) we have ra = rb ⇐⇒b = {±a}, using RS2
for “⇒”.
We shall see that there is a very rich theory of root systems, and that in turn is a powerful
tool in the the study of connected compact Lie groups (with finite center). To make the
link, we need to verify the Integrality Axiom for (X(T)Q, Φ(G, T)) when ZG is finite. In
fact, we shall do much more: we will show ℓa(X(T)) ⊂Z without any restriction on ZG.

Definition 20.8. For (V, Φ) a root system, its Weyl group is the subgroup

W(Φ) = ⟨rc⟩c∈Φ ⊂GL(V).

This is finite since it consists of linear automorphisms that permute the finite spanning set
Φ of V.

Remark 20.9. Later, we’ll show that naturally

W (Φ (G, T)) = W(G, T)

for any G with finite center. In fact, we’ll do much better: the general assertion we will
later establish (without hypotheses on ZG) is that W(G, T) is generated by the reflections
ra for a ∈Φ(G, T); this is not at all evident.

ℓa ∈X(T)∗
Q? Our goal is to show ℓa(X(T)) ⊂Z (with no finiteness hypothesis on ZG), so
in particular ℓa(Φ(G, T)) ⊂Z as desired.
It is easy to see that the bilinear pairing

X(T) × X∗(T) →End(S1) = Z
(χ, λ) �→χ ◦λ = ⟨χ, λ⟩

is a perfect pairing of finite free Z-modules. Thus, we seek a homomorphism of Lie groups

a∨: S1 →T

so that

ra(x) = x −⟨x, a∨⟩a

for all x ∈X(T). Since

ra(a) = −a,

cases, the condition is then that the isogeny a : T →S1 of 1-dimensional tori factors
through the squaring endomorphism of S1, which is exactly the condition that #(ker a)
divides 2. For SU(2) this is fine (the kernel has order 2) and for SO(3) the root viewed
as a character is even an isomorphism. In general, one can check the construction for
(Ga, T′

a ⊂T; see Appendix R
for details.

Remark 21.4. For groups G with finite center, it is an important fact that the root system
will characterize G up to isogeny. We will come back to this later in the course (but won’t
get into its proof, which requires finer structural work with semisimple Lie algebras).
The notion of root datum (which is more general than that of a root system, and includes
data associated to coroots) refines this to characterize G up to isomorphism with no
restriction on ZG. We will also return to this matter later, after we have developed some
structure theory for root systems and seek to apply it to get structural results for compact
connected Lie groups.

Warning 21.5.

�
People usually write Φ to denote a root system, rather than the more
accurate notation (V, Φ).

For any root system (V, Φ), we saw the reflection ra : V ≃V negating a ∈Φ is uniquely
determined by (V, Φ), and deduced that ra = rb if and only if b = ±a. (Recall that in this
course root systems are always reduced.) But ra(x) = x −ℓa(x)a, so if we define

a∨:= ℓa ∈V∗−{0}


96
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

(and call this the coroot attached to a ∈Φ) then the fact that ra = r−a forces ℓ−a = −ℓa.
Hence, we obtain in general for any root system that

(−a)∨= −(a∨).

Whereas the reflection ra determines a only up to a sign, the coroot determines the root on
the nose:

Lemma 21.6. For a, b ∈Φ, if a∨= b∨then a = b.

Proof. Note that ker (a∨) =: Ha ⊂V is the hyperplane where ra = 1. Therefore, Vra=1 =
Vrb=1. This implies ra = rb (by using Lemma 19.8), so b = ±a. But (−a)∨= −(a∨) ̸= a∨

(as a∨̸= 0), so b ̸= −a and hence b = a.
□

Definition 21.7. Define Φ∨⊂V∗−{0} as the image of

Φ �→V∗

a �→a∨.

For a∨∈Φ∨a coroot, define ra∨:= (ra)∗: V∗≃V∗. Observe that ra∨is a reflection on
V∗(since it has order 2 and −1 as an eigenvalue with multiplicity 1, inherited from ra since
passing to the dual endomorphism has no effect on the characteristic polynomial).

Lemma 21.8. For x∗∈V∗, we have

ra∨(x∗) = x∗−⟨a, x∗⟩a∨.
(21.1)

This is proved by expanding out the definitions, using that ra∨(x∗) = x∗◦ra.

Theorem 21.9. The datum (V∗, Φ∨) and {ra∨}a∨∈Φ∨is a root system. Further,

ra∨(b∨) = (ra(b))∨.

Before proving this result, we make some observations. The data (V∗, Φ∨) is called the
dual root system. By using (21.1), we see that (a∨)∨= a.
Further, when k = Q or k = R and we choose a W(Φ)-invariant inner product ⟨·|·⟩to
identify V∗with V, we get

a∨=
2a
⟨a|a⟩.

This formula is visibly not generally additive in a (as we see in some of the explicit rank-2
examples earlier for some pairs of roots whose sum is a root).
In the purely combinatorial setting, we defined W(Φ) as a group generated by reflections
in the roots, and this was easily seen to be finite. But in the Lie theory setting, we
cooked up reflections in the finite group W(G, T), and it is not at all evident if those
generate W(G, T). That is, for ZG finite (so Φ(G, T) is a root system) we have the inclusion
W(Φ(G, T)) ⊂W(G, T) but equality isn’t obvious.
Over the course of the next two lectures we will use honest Euclidean geometry and
some new concepts (such as Weyl chambers) to prove that W(G, T) is generated by the
reflections in the roots (without any finiteness restriction on ZG).

Sketch of Theorem 21.9. The full proof of this theorem is in Appendix S. The two key things
to prove are:

(i) Φ∨spans V∗;


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
97

(ii) (ra∨) (b∨) = (ra(b))∨(so the dual reflections really do preserve Φ∨).
Here are the main ideas for these two parts:

(i) If k = Q or R (or in general any ordered field,) one can build a W(Φ)-invariant
positive-definite quadratic form q via averaging. Consider the associated (non-
degenerate) symmetric bilinear form ⟨·|·⟩q on V. Using this to identify V∗with V,
the coroot a∨∈V∗is identified with (2/q(a))a which is a k×-multiple of a. Thus,
the spanning is clear because Φ spans V.
For general k a miracle happens. Define the Q-subspace V0 = QΦ ⊂V, which
is stable by all ra (using the Integrality Axiom!). The miracle is that the natural
surjection k ⊗Q V0 ↠V is an isomorphism, so one can reduce to the settled case
k = Q!
(ii) To show ra∨(b∨) = b∨◦ra = ra(b)∨, one does not just blindly crank out r(ra(b)) and
hope for the best. Instead, the key is that one needs a formula for r(ra(b)). In fact,
r(ra(b)) = rarbr−1
a . This holds because of the uniqueness of the reflection associated
to a root and the fact that ra acts on (V, Φ) (more generally: any automorphism of a
root system (V, Φ) must be compatible with the associated reflections!).
□

Remark 21.10. In [Bou2], the Euclidean geometry aspects of reflection groups are de-
veloped in the setting of Hilbert spaces without assuming finite-dimensionality. There
are no reasons to care about this beyond the finite-dimensional case, so assume finite-
dimensionality without any concern when you read [Bou2].

W (G, T) = ⟨ra⟩a∈Φ ⊂GL(X(T)Q).

The crucial concept needed in this proof will be Weyl chambers, defined in terms of
connected components of complements of a (locally) finite collection of hyperplanes in
a finite-dimensional R-vector space. It is exactly at this step that we really have to finally
use the ground field k = R in our work on root systems, and not just use pure algebra:
defining such connected components is very painful purely in terms of algebra since being
on certain sides of some of the hyperplanes, as can be described algebraically, might force
one to be on a specific side of another hyperplane (that is, we can’t just specify which side

BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

of each hyperplane we want to be on and expect the resulting condition to be non-empty).
This is where topology over R is a very useful tool.

22. EQUALITY OF TWO NOTIONS OF WEYL GROUP I

Last time, we defined the dual (V∗, Φ∨) of a root system (V, Φ) and saw that for n ≥2,
An−1 := Φ (SU(n)) is self-dual. In terms of the classification of irreducible roots systems,
Dn := Φ (SO(2n)) is also self-dual, whereas Cn := Φ (Sp(n)) turns out to be dual to
Bn := Φ (SO(2n + 1)) for n ≥1. We’ll come back to the computation of these next time.
There is an important link between root systems over R and Lie algebras, as follows. For
(G, T) with ZG finite, the dual root system over R has underlying vector space

(X (T)R)∗=
�
X (T)∗�

R = X∗(T)R ≃t =: Lie(T)

by assigning to each cocharacter (S1
f−→T) ∈X∗(T) the image under

d f (e) : Lie(S1) →t

of a chosen “standard basis” of Lie(S1) arising from a fixed choice of uniformization of S1

(say as R/Z or R/2πZ). This defines the R-linear map X∗(T)R →t, and to show it is an
isomorphism we first observe that this construction is compatible with direct products in
T and is functorial in T. Thus, using an isomorphism T ≃(S1)r reduces the verification
to the case T = S1 that is elementary. This isomorphism X∗(T)R ≃t works without any
hypotheses of ZG; we only mentioned finiteness of ZG to make a link with root systems.

Remark 22.1. For any root system (V, Φ), last time we also defined the Cartan numbers

na,b = ℓa(b) = a∨(b).

Likewise, we have for the dual root system

na∨,b∨=

using V ≃(V∗)∗.
In other words, the Cartan matrix for Φ (whose rows and columns are labeled by a
common fixed enumeration of the set of roots, or really a specific subset called a basis
that we’ll discuss later, and whose ab-entry is na,b for a, b ∈Φ). is transpose to the Cartan
matrix of Φ∨(when we enumerate the coroots in accordance with how we do it for the
roots).

22.1. Weyl Chambers. We now draw various examples of rank-2 root systems over R and

root c, we defined the “root hyperplane”

H−c = Hc := ker(c∨) = Vrc=1.

The connected components K of V −(∪c∈ΦHc) are called the Weyl chambers.

In each of the above cases, we see by inspection #K = #W(Φ). This is no accident: the
action of W = W(Φ) on V certainly permutes the set of Weyl chambers (since it permutes
the collection of the root hyperplanes, as those are intrinsic to the root system), and we’ll
see soon that the action is simply transitive.
In the Lie theory setting, W(G, T) also acts on X(T)R and permutes the collection of
root hyperplanes (again, since these are intrinsically determined by the roots), so W(G, T)
permutes the set of Weyl chambers (with no assumptions on ZG, such as finiteness).


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
101

We want to prove W(G, T) = ⟨ra⟩a∈Φ. This would then imply W(G, T) = W(Φ(G, T))
when ZG is finite (so (X(T)Q, Φ(G, T)) is actually a root system). In fact, we’ll show that
W(G, T) is generated by a specific set of reflections.

Definition 22.6. For a Weyl chamber K of a root system, a wall of K is a hyperplane H = Hc
so that intH(K ∩H) ̸= ∅.

Theorem 22.7. For ZG finite, W (G, T) = ⟨rc⟩Hc∈wall(K) for a fixed choice of Weyl chamber K.
In particular, W(G, T) = ⟨rc⟩c∈Φ =: W(Φ).

To prove Theorem 22.7, we need the following result that is part of HW8 (with hints):

Lemma 22.8. For a root system (V, Φ) over R and a Weyl chamber K, the following hold:

(1) ∂V(K) = �
H∈wall(K)
�
H ∩K
�

(2) intV(K) = K
(3) K = �
H∈wall(K) (H+K), where H+K denotes the connected component of V −H contain-
ing K.

Remark 22.9. The theory of Coxeter groups can be used to infer a presentation of W(Φ)
using Theorem 22.7. For example, this yields for Sn = W (Φ (SU(n))) a presentation as a
group generated by the adjacent transpositions (12) , (23) , . . . , (n −1, n), subject to certain
braid relations.

Proof of Theorem 22.7. To start, we pass to the dual root system, as we may do without any
harm. (This will be important when proving Lemma 22.11 below.) Thus, now the underly-
ing R-vector space is t on which W = NG(T)/T acts through the adjoint representation of
G on g. Let W′ := ⟨rc⟩Hc∈wall(K) ⊂W(G, T).
We’ll show W′ acts transitively on the set of Weyl chambers and W(G, T) has only trivial
isotropy on any chamber (or equivalently on one chamber, once W′-transitivity for the
action on the set of chambers is proved). The first of these says that W′ is big enough and
the second (which means w.K = K ⇒w = 1 for w ∈W(G, T)) says that the ambient group
W(G, T) is small enough. Putting these together clearly implies W′ = W(G, T). Thus, the
proof follows from Lemma 22.10 and Lemma 22.11 below.
□

Lemma 22.10. W′ acts transitively on the Weyl chambers.

Proof. Choose Weyl chambers K′ ̸= K and pick y ∈K′, x ∈K. Pick w′ ∈W′ so that the
distance |w′.y −x|is minimal. We’ll show w′.y ∈K which implies w′(K′) = K (since if two
connected components meet they must be the same).
Assume, for the sake of contradiction that w′.y /∈K (so w′.K′ ̸= K). Fix a W(G, T)-
invariant inner product on V. By renaming w′.y as y and w′.K′ ̸= K as K′, we can assume
|y −x| ≤|w′.y −x| for all w′ ∈W′, yet with y ∈K′ ̸= K. Consider the line segment [y, x].
It is an exercise to use Lemma 22.8 to deduce that [y, x] crosses some wall Ha of K, so x
and y lie on opposite sides of H (neither lies in H since they don’t lie in any wall of any
Weyl chamber). Thus, ra ∈W′. We will look at ra(y).
Using the orthogonal decomposition V = H ⊕H⊥to decompose y and x into their
components along H and along the line H⊥, applying ra = rH to y has no effect on the
H-component but negates the H⊥-component. By considering H⊥-components, we claim
that ra(y) is closer to x than y, which would be a contradiction to the minimizing property
we have arranged for y (as ra ∈W′). Working along the line H⊥in which the components


102
BRIAN CONRAD AND AARON LANDESMAN

of x and y are nonzero (as these don’t live in any of the walls of any of the Weyl chambers)
and lie on opposite sides of the origin (as x and y are on opposite sides of H), this is
essentially saying that if we consider a negative number and a positive number, and we
negate the negative number, then it becomes closer to the positive number. This completes
the proof of transitivity of W′ on the set of Weyl chambers.
□

To prove Theorem 22.7, it only remains to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 22.11. For w ∈W(G, T) = NG(T)/T such that w.K = K, we necessarily have w = 1.

Proof. Consider K ⊂X∗(T)R = t. Pick x ∈K. By openness of K in t and density of Q in R,
we can approximate x so that x ∈X∗(T)Q. Letting Γ be the finite cyclic group generated
by w, consider ∑γ∈Γ γ.x ∈K, membership since K is a cone (really use Lemma 22.8(3)).
This sum is fixed by w and lies in X∗(T)Q. We can scale by Z+ so x ∈X∗(T). This yields
a map x : S1 →T which is centralized by a representative g ∈NG(T) of w. Next time
we’ll use an argument with roots to show that the centralizer of x is equal to T and use
this to deduce g ∈ZG(T) = T, so the element w ∈W(G, T) represented by g is trivial, as
desired.
□

23. EQUALITY OF TWO NOTIONS OF WEYL GROUP II

Last time we were confronted with the situation of a cocharacter x : S1 →T corre-
spondng to a point in X∗(T) ⊂X∗(T)R = t lying in some Weyl chamber (and hence outside
all of the (co)root hyperplanes) and g ∈NG(T) such that gxg−1 = x in X∗(T), and we
wanted to show that g ∈T (so the class w ∈W(G, T) of g is trivial, which was our real
goal at the end of last time, when showing the simple transitivity of the action of W(G, T)
on the set of Weyl chambers in the dual root system). Note that the action of W(G, T) on t
via the surjection NG(T) ↠W(G, T) is induced by the AdG-action on g (differentiating at
the identity for the effect of conjugation, much as X∗(T) �→t is defined by differentiation
of cocharacters at the identity).
We’ll now show that the x-centralizer ZG(x) = ZG
�
x(S1)
�
in G (which clearly contains
g) is just T, so g ∈T as desired. Since x(S1) is a torus, the centralizer ZG(x) = ZG(x(S1))
is a connected compact Lie group that contains T (as x(S1) ⊂T), and T is visibly maximal
in here since it is even maximal in G. We assume ZG(x) ̸= T and seek a contradiction.
By connectedness, if the containment T ⊂ZG(x) is not an equality then the subspace
Lie(ZG(x)) ⊂g is strictly larger than t, forcing its complexification to have a nontrivial
T-weight. But the T-action on gC is completely reducible with tC as the weight space for
the trivial weight and the other weights being the roots, each with a 1-dimensional weight
space. Thus, Lie (ZG(x))C must contain the “root line” (gC)a for some a ∈Φ(G, T).

But Lie (ZG(x))C = (gC)x(S1)=1, so (gC)a ⊂(gC)x(S1)=1. Since T acts on the a-root line
through scaling against a : T →S1, and x(S1) ⊂T, it follows that x(S1) acts on (gC)a
through scalar multiplication against its image

a(x(S1)) = (a ◦x)(S1) ⊂S1 ⊂C×

under a. Hence, the containment in (gC)x(S1)=1 forces a ◦x ∈End(S1) = Z to vanish,
which is to say a ◦x : S1 →S1 is trivial.


MATH 210C. COMPACT LIE GROUPS
103

Thus, x(S1) ⊂(ker a)0 =: Ta, so the cocharacter x ∈X∗(T) ⊂t lies in Lie(Ta) =
ker(da(e)) that is exactly the kernel of a viewed as a linear functional on X∗(T)R. This
is exactly the (co)root hyperplane Ha∨in the dual root system via the identitifcation
a = (a∨)∨. But x ∈K, so x does not lie in any (co)root hyperplanes in the dual root system!
This is a contradiction, so indeed ZG(x) = T as desired, completing the proof.
Passing back to the original root system, we have now finally proved

W(G, T) = ⟨ra⟩a∈Φ =: W (Φ(G, T))
